The advisory size and reach report-only rules — `function-length`, `parameters`, `nesting-depth`, `decision-points` and `dead-export` — are `.warning` under every profile and can never refuse; their thresholds come from `lint.rules.<name>.max` and default to 80 lines, 6 parameters, nesting 4, 10 decision points, and no caller anywhere in the project respectively.
- [LINT-3] `mylang lint --accept` writes the baseline and ratchet stores under `.mylang/lint/`, keyed on the checker's resolved declaration identity rather than message text. A baseline row `<module>|<qualified>` marks one existing finding as accepted debt: under `--gate` a blocking finding with a row is reported at warning severity and does not refuse, while a finding without a row refuses. A ratchet row `<qualified> <ceiling>` records a per-declaration ceiling for a threshold rule that may only shrink. A run without `--accept` never writes, prunes or lowers a store, baseline rows are sorted so the committed file is merge-friendly, and a store file containing merge conflict markers is refused rather than merged.
- [LINT-4] `integration-tests` (`L_TEST_CAPABILITY`) reports a discovered test whose declared parameters request `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock` or `Entropy` and whose module does not match the rule's `modules` globs (default `*_integration`, `integration.*`), because such a test is an integration test and belongs in an integration module. It reports under every profile and never blocks; a reasoned `exempt` entry or a matching module glob leaves the test alone.
- [LINT-5] The tests-reachability index built for `mylang tests-for` is shared: every `mylang lint` finding that names a declaration carries the discovered tests that reach it as a related note (a rule that set its own related note keeps it, and a declaration no test reaches gets none), and `mylang list-interfaces` prints a `tests(N)` column on each exported callable when bodies were checked, where `N` is the number of discovered tests that reach it directly or transitively.
- [LINT-6] The advisory section-9 report rules ship under every profile and can never block: `record-size` (`L_RECORD_SIZE`, a record or class over `rules.record-size.max`, default 7 fields), `file-length` (`L_FILE_LENGTH`, a module over `rules.file-length.max`, default 1000 non-blank non-comment lines), `bool-ops-per-condition` (`L_BOOL_OPS`, a single branch or loop condition combining more than `rules.bool-ops-per-condition.max` `and`/`or` operators, default 3), `naming` (`L_NAMING`, a function, parameter, local, record, union or domain that is not snake_case, PascalCase or off the vague-name list), `repeated-literal` (`L_REPEATED_LITERAL`, an integer literal other than `0`/`1` appearing at least three times in one module), `same-type-adjacent-params` (`L_SAME_TYPE_ADJACENT_PARAMS`, adjacent parameters sharing a non-capability type), `duplicate-body` (`L_DUPLICATE_BODY`, two or more checked functions with an identical normalized body, never blocking) and `output-in-library` (`L_OUTPUT_IN_LIBRARY`, a function whose inferred effects include `output` in a module outside `rules.output-in-library.modules`).

## Collection and statement surface

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list push(value), len(), indexing, and indexed assignment preserve independent values across bindings by default; shared aliases remain only under `--legacy-sharing`.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-2] Record construction names its type and fields — `Box[i64](value=42)`, `Empty()` — covering generic records, empty records declared with `pass`, and trailing argument commas.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-3] Generic managed list methods and indexing preserve abstract element type checking.

- [SURFACE-STMT-7] Condition expressions admit record constructors inside nested call arguments; record and field names remain case-sensitive identifiers.
- [SURFACE-STMT-8] Record constructor syntax accepts nested generic type arguments, including List fields initialized with contextually typed empty lists.
- [SURFACE-STMT-9] A newline separates postfix call suffixes from completed expressions outside parentheses, while parentheses permit multiline calls and suffixes.

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-4] Managed list methods reject invalid argument counts, unknown methods, mismatched element types, and noninteger indices.

## Prelude types and contextual constructors

- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-1] List<T> and Result<T,E> are available without imports or explicit declarations and use existing managed-list and fallible-result semantics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-2] Empty lists and Ok/Err payload constructors use expected binding, return, and concrete call-argument types.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-3] Record fields and managed list elements propagate their expected types into nested empty list expressions.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-4] Ok(()) constructs successful Result<unit,E> values with normal handling obligations.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-5] Generic List and Result expressions preserve symbolic type parameters through empty list creation, mutation, and Ok construction.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-6] Contextual constructors reject missing or incompatible expected types, invalid generic arity, and forbidden managed capability elements.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-7] Explicitly declared List and Result types retain their own nominal identities and contextual constructors.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-8] Builtin List and Result types share canonical identity across imported module signatures without imports of a prelude module.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-9] A module-local List declaration shadows the prelude in that module while unrelated modules continue to resolve the builtin List.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-10] Module-local functions named Ok or Err take precedence over contextual constructors in both ordinary and generic bodies, without spurious constructor diagnostics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-11] A list method's operands are expected types too: `append`, `remove` and `index` expect the element type, `insert` an integer position and the element type, and `extend` the list type, so `rows.append([])` infers the empty literal as `rows: list[list[int]] = []` does and `octets.append(200)` on a `list[u8]` types the literal as `u8`; `bytes([])` and `byte_buffer([])` infer `list[int]`.
- [SCHEMATIC-INPUTS-1] The schematic host provides named circuit, pinout, and interface documents through io.inputs; typed Document values carry the document identity and an owned immutable byte copy loaded through bounded host blocks. Older guests declaring only the original scalar byte callbacks remain supported by the provider.
- [SCHEMATIC-INPUTS-2] Named input providers reject absent host documents instead of issuing unusable handles.
- [HOST-GUEST-POLICY-1] A host application names the code generator and the accounting its guest is built with, and runs that guest under exactly the ceilings the build can enforce. `.qbe` passes `--qbe PATH`, which pins the assembler; `.llvm` passes `--backend llvm` and pins none, so the driver resolves the release code generator built into it. `.steps` accounting passes `--step-accounting` and bounds one invocation with `--max-steps=`; `.frames` accounting passes `--max-frames=N` instead and bounds one invocation with that same call-depth ceiling and a `--timeout=` wall clock, counting no instructions. The invocation's ceilings are derived from the policy rather than restated by the caller, so a guest is never handed a ceiling whose accounting its build dropped, and a caller's own tighter ceiling is the one the runtime reads first.
- [SCHEMATIC-RUNNER-POLICY-1] `schematic-svg`, its `--dev-session` and its whole-fixture test build the guest the way a release `mylang build` does — the LLVM code generator at `-O3`, no instruction accounting — instead of pinning the debug assembler and counting every instruction. `--backend qbe` selects QBE and drops the release path's LLVM controls, `--llvm-opt` and `--lto` override the rest, and `--bounded` restores instruction accounting and its `--max-steps=` ceiling.
- [SCHEMATIC-RUNNER-BOUNDS-1] A runaway guest under the shipped policy is still stopped: unbounded recursion trapping at the call-depth ceiling, a loop that never leaves trapping at the wall clock, and a runaway allocation trapping at the byte ceiling. None of the three publishes SVG.

- [MODULE-DOT-1] Direct import aliases accept dot-qualified function and type references, including generic records and imported domain methods; local list methods retain their meanings.
- [MODULE-DOT-2] Dot-qualified imported functions can be passed as values, and generic function bodies resolve dotted module type references through their declaring module.
- [MODULE-DOT-3] Dotted qualification preserves private-symbol, private-domain-function, and import-alias-shadowing checks.
- [MODULE-DOT-4] Dotted module parsing, namespace normalization, checking, and execution release all allocations on allocation failure.

- [SCHEMATIC-MODEL-1] The schematic entry parses named document handles, builds one validated schematic value, and passes it to rendering; normal and collection-stress execution produce identical SVG and release all managed roots.
- [MODULE-DOT-5] Semantic caching recognizes dotted imported function values and keeps callback type catalogs fresh across repeated requests and imported function edits.
- [MODULE-DOT-6] An import admits the full dotted module path (`a.b.name`) and its local alias — the explicit `as` name, or the trailing segment (`b.name`) when there is none — as qualified function and type references. A bare prefix that does not continue a registered module path (`shared` when `shared.geometry` is imported) is not reserved, so a local or parameter may reuse it; only a segment that continues the path is joined. A multi-segment qualified name normalizes by splitting at its last dot.

## Python-oriented core values

- [PYTHON-CORE-1] Prelude List, Result and Option have one nominal identity shared across modules; unrelated user nominal declarations remain distinct.
- [PYTHON-CORE-2] Unit and Result<unit/int,int> entry functions adapt to the scalar runner status convention while retaining their ordinary guest signatures. The synthesized wrapper is not a source declaration, so no public interface names it and a project with such an entry emits native units like any other.
- [PYTHON-CORE-3] Representable unsuffixed integer constants inherit numeric contexts; explicit suffixes retain their types and out-of-range constants are rejected.
- [PYTHON-CORE-4] Decimal float literals and ordinary arithmetic/comparisons use binary64 numeric semantics, including equal signed zeros and unordered NaNs.
- [NUM-LITERAL-1] Integer literals take Python's spellings: decimal, `0x`/`0X` hexadecimal, `0o`/`0O` octal and `0b`/`0B` binary, digits in either case, with single underscores between digits and one directly after the base prefix (`1_000`, `0xDEAD_BEEF`, `0x_FF`, `0b1010_1010`). A spelling denotes the same value as its decimal form and keeps its type rules. A leading, trailing or doubled underscore (`1__0`, `1_`, `0_x`), a base prefix with no digit (`0x`) and a digit outside the base (`0b12`, `0o8`) are located `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` errors. A decimal integer may not start with a zero unless every digit is zero (`0`, `00`, `0_0` and `0u8` are legal), as in Python 3: `0755` is an `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` error whose message says to write `0o755` for octal or `755` for decimal, rather than silently meaning 755. Float literals keep leading zeros ([NUM-LITERAL-3]).
- [NUM-LITERAL-2] An integer width suffix (`i8` `i16` `i32` `i64` `u8` `u16` `u32` `u64`) follows the last digit directly and combines with every base: `0xFFu8`, `0b1010u16`, `0o17i32`, `1_000u32`. A suffix begins at the first byte that is not a digit of the base, and the suffix letters `i` and `u` are digits of no base, so a hexadecimal literal never swallows its suffix; an underscore between the digits and the suffix (`1_u8`) is a doubled or trailing underscore, and an unknown suffix stays `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX`. A `j` suffix (`1j`, `1.5j`) is rejected with a message that mylang has no complex numbers.
- [NUM-LITERAL-3] Float literals take Python's spellings: a leading dot (`.5`), a trailing dot (`5.`, `1.e2`), exponents (`1e10`, `2.5E-3`) and single underscores between digits in the mantissa and the exponent (`1_000.5`, `1e1_0`); leading zeros are allowed (`007.5`). `.5` is a float only where no operand ends directly before the dot, so `x.5` and `f().5` keep their field-access reading, and `5.name` keeps `5` and `.name` apart instead of forming a malformed float. A float takes no integer suffix and a hexadecimal, octal or binary spelling is never a float.
- [NUM-LITERAL-4] An unsuffixed integer literal in any base takes the integer type its context expects when its value fits it, in `const` initializers as in typed locals, fields, arguments and returns: `const MASK: u32 = 4278190080`, `const LOW: u8 = 0xFF`, `A + 1` beside a `u32` constant. Compile-time function bodies use the same rule for annotated bindings, call arguments, returns and assignments. A value outside the expected type stays an `E_INTEGER_RANGE` error, and an unexpected context leaves the literal `int`. The hint that gives a literal a constant's type inspects declaration policy before resolving a known constant and looks names up silently: a name that is not a constant, such as the module in `helpers.Kind.B`, leaves no diagnostic, so comparing against a module-qualified enum case beside module constants checks as before.
- [NUM-LITERAL-5] Malformed numeric spellings are reported once, at the literal, with a stable code: `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` for a misplaced underscore, a missing or invalid digit and a decimal leading zero, `E_FLOAT_LITERAL` for a malformed float, exponent or suffix, `E_INTEGER_RANGE` for a value that does not fit its type, and `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX` for an unknown suffix. No operator is added: `**`, `<<` and `>>` keep the [PARSER-HINT-10] alternatives.
- [NUM-LITERAL-6] `mylang fmt` reproduces a numeric literal byte for byte, so `0xFF`, `1_000` and `.5` are never rewritten to `255`, `1000` or `0.5`, and the checked constants in an interface listing keep the spelling the declaration wrote.
- [NUM-LITERAL-7] An array length `[N]T` and an explicit const generic length argument (`make[i64, 0x2](values)`) accept any unsuffixed integer spelling: `[16]u8`, `[0x10]u8`, `[0o20]u8`, `[0b1_0000]u8`, `[1_024]u8`. Every spelling of one value names the same type, and an interface listing prints the decimal form. A width suffix (`[2u8]`), a malformed spelling and a decimal leading zero (`[02]`) are rejected with a message naming the rule, as `E_ARRAY_LENGTH` for an array and `E_GENERIC_LENGTH` for a const generic argument; the host and 1048576-element bounds are unchanged.

## Surface syntax

- [PYTHON-1] Sources use `def`/`record`/`class` heads, colon and space-indented blocks, `#` comments, implicit continuation inside delimiters, and source-preserving virtual block delimiters. A suite opens at the `:` that ends its line and closes at its dedent; `{` and `}` are only a dictionary literal, so a brace-bodied declaration is rejected. A suite's reported bytes end at the last token it wrote, so the blank lines and comments before the next declaration belong to neither, and a function's measured length and navigation excerpt cover only the function. Missing blocks, inconsistent dedents, and indentation tabs are diagnosed. Indentation is the only block syntax, so `fmt` returns the text a program already has.
- [PYTHON-2] Functions support ordinary mutable assignment, annotated bindings, `True`/`False`/`None`, `and`/`or`/`not`, `pass`, `elif`, omitted unit return annotations, `int` as i64, and square type parameters whose omitted requirement defaults to copy. `pass` spells an empty suite wherever one is written, including a record with no fields and a domain with no members. A `;` separates two statements written on one line, as it does in Python.
- [ATTR-1] A declaration may carry a fixed-vocabulary attribute on the line above it: @deprecated("reason"), @test, or @allow("rule", reason="…"). The parser rejects any other @name with E_UNKNOWN_ATTRIBUTE and a repeated attribute on one declaration with E_DUPLICATE_ATTRIBUTE; the contract clauses @requires, @ensures and @invariant (CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1) are the attributes that may repeat. Attributes are part of the declaration header and the formatter round-trips them.
- [ATTR-2] @test makes a zero-parameter bool function a discovered test regardless of its name or export flag; mylang test, the native test schema and the W2 test rules agree on that set, and @test is reported in list-declarations and list-interfaces --contracts.
- [ATTR-3] A call to a @deprecated declaration from another module reports W_DEPRECATED naming the reason; the attribute participates in the declaration and project interface fingerprints, so removing a declaration deprecated in the baseline is an interface-compatible change once api-diff exists.
- [ATTR-4] @allow("rule", reason="…") on a declaration suppresses that lint rule for findings on the declaration and replaces the # lint: allow comment form; a reason is still mandatory (L_WAIVER_REASON). The comment form is still accepted alongside the attribute this round.
- [DOC-1] A triple- or single-quoted string literal that is the first statement of a module or of a function body is that declaration's docstring; it is inert, contributes no effect, and later standalone strings stay ordinary discarded expressions. list-interfaces shows its first line.
- [DOC-2] L_UNDOCUMENTED_EXPORT reports an exported function, record, union or domain with no docstring, one shorter than min_chars (12 by default, counted over non-whitespace characters), or one that merely restates the declaration name. It reports under agent and blocks under strict.
- [PY-RECORD-1] `record Name:` is the canonical declaration head for a nominal value record with annotated fields and named construction. `record` remains an ordinary identifier outside declaration position, so variables and parameters may still be named `record`; `class Name:` remains accepted as the compatibility spelling during the migration window.
- [PYTHON-3] Value records use `record` (or the `class` compatibility spelling) with annotated fields and named construction; square-bracket type applications and `match`/`case` preserve contextual variant names and underscore payload discards for semantic resolution. Every explicit type argument uses square brackets, including `todo[T]()`, `wrap[D](v)`, `codec_encode[D](v)` and the capability and resource operations.
- [PY-LAMBDA-1] `lambda name, ...: expression` is an expression-bodied spelling for the existing noncapturing function value. Its parameter and result types come from the expected `def(...) -> ... effects(...)` callback type, and invocation uses the same checked callback path as a named function value.
- [PY-LAMBDA-2] A lambda without an expected callback type is rejected. Its expression must match the expected result and exact effect ceiling; it may read its parameters and module constants but cannot capture a function local, whose diagnostic recommends a top-level function or explicit state parameter.

- [PY-VARIANT-1] `Type.case(value)` constructs an enum or tagged-union variant carrying its declared payload; `Type.case` without parentheses stays the payload-free construction and contextual `Ok`/`Err`/`Some`/`None` are unchanged. The payload evaluates once and `match`/`case` patterns keep the same spelling.
- [PY-GENERIC-1] `f[T](x)` applies explicit type arguments to a generic function, constant lengths included (`first[i64, 2](values)`), and a member call takes them the same way: `D.hidden[U](x)` on a domain method, `m.D.make[U](x)` on an imported one, and `m.make[i64, 2](v)` on an imported free function. An argument may name any type the lexer spells as a keyword, capabilities included: `hold[clock_cap]()`. A square-bracket application in call position names a function when the base is a declared function and a generic record otherwise, so `Box[T](field=v)` construction is unchanged and the nominal type catalog never resolves a function application as a type.
- [PY-GENERIC-2] Explicit call type arguments reach the same instance as inference, and the wrong number of arguments or an undeclared base name is rejected.
- [PY-FLOAT-1] `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` are prelude operations on `float`, so guest code needs no `u64` bit pattern. Each takes one float and answers a float; a non-float argument is rejected, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows the operation.
- [PY-WILDCARD-1] `case _` in a `match` statement covers every variant no earlier arm named, discarding the payload of each one it covers.
- [PY-WILDCARD-2] A wildcard arm must be the last arm, must cover at least one variant, and is rejected when it would drop a result-bearing payload; that variant needs its own arm.
- [PY-SUBSCRIPT-1] A subscript index may begin with a parenthesis: `values[(n - 1) % 3]` is an index, because a square-bracket type application never contains a parenthesized group.
- [PY-RESERVED-1] `len` and `replace` are intrinsics only at the head of a call. Both name ordinary declarations, record fields, keyword arguments, members, exports and imported module members, so `strings.replace(...)`, `pool.len(p)`, `text.replace(old, new)` and a field named `len` are all spellable.
- [PY-LOOP-1] A `while True` loop with no `break` targeting it never falls through, so a function whose body ends in one needs no unreachable trailing return.
- [PY-PRELUDE-1] The `bytes` and `float` prelude types are registered wherever their names are written in source, including a variant payload, a record field and a bare annotation, rather than only where a literal or conversion appears.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-1] `sum(xs)` adds every element of a `list[int]` or `list[float]` in order, starting from zero of the element type; an empty list sums to zero and any other element type is rejected.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-2] `any(xs)` and `all(xs)` fold a `list[bool]` with `or` and `and`, stopping as soon as `any` sees `True` or `all` sees `False`; an empty list answers `False` and `True` respectively.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-1] `min` and `max` take a list of `int`, `float` or `str` values, or two scalars of one type, and answer an element. Text order compares the stored Unicode scalars directly and contributes no managed allocation, so a text extrema fold is accepted under `noalloc`. An empty list has no smallest element, so it traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` at the call site.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-2] `min(a, b)` and `max(a, b)` of two scalars evaluate each argument exactly once, left to right, like any other call: `max(len(xs), push(mut xs) + len(xs))` runs `push` once. The second argument used to be evaluated for the comparison and again for the answer.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-1] `sorted(xs)` returns a new ascending list of integer, `float`, `str` or `bytes` elements, leaving the input list and every alias of it unchanged. It uses the same stable `O(n log n)` native merge engine as in-place sorting with one reusable work buffer. Equal elements and signed zeros keep their input order, NaNs follow every ordered float, and `str` values order by Unicode code point, matching `sort.compare_strs`.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-KEY-1] `sorted(xs, key=callback, reverse=flag)` copies the list and sorts the copy exactly as `values.sort(key=callback, reverse=flag)` sorts in place (LIST-SORT-KEY-1): any ordered key, each computed once in input order, stable with and without `reverse`. The input remains unchanged, the list is positional, and `key` and `reverse` are its only named arguments, both optional and keyword-only.
- [PRELUDE-ABS-1] `abs` is total for `int`: it answers the magnitude and traps `R_OVERFLOW` on the one value with no positive counterpart, `-9223372036854775808`. A `float` argument still lowers to the prelude float operation.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-1] `round(value)` takes a `float` and answers the nearest integral `float`, with halves rounded away from zero, matching `math.round`. Not-a-number and the infinities pass through unchanged.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-2] `round(value, ndigits)` takes a `float` and an integer and answers the `float` nearest the exact binary value rounded to `ndigits` decimal digits, a negative count rounding left of the point, with halves away from zero as `PRELUDE-ROUND-1` rounds: `round(2.675, 2)` is `2.67` because the binary value lies below the half, while `round(0.125, 2)` is `0.13` where CPython's ties-to-even gives `0.12`. As in CPython a count above 323 answers the value, one below -308 a zero of its sign, and not-a-number and the infinities pass through.
- [PRELUDE-ITERATION-1] `reversed(xs)` answers a new list of a list or string `xs` from last to first and is also a `for` source; `zip(a, b)` is a `for` source that walks two lists in step and stops at the shorter one; `list(...)` copies a list and materialises `list(range(end))` or `list(range(start, end))`.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-1] Every prelude builtin answers only when no local binding, declared function or module top-level name claims it, the same shadowing rule the other prelude operations follow.
- [PRELUDE-KEYS-1] `list`, `sorted`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` of a dictionary read its keys, and of a `keys()` or `values()` view that view's snapshot, in insertion order as a `for` loop visits them; a set reads its elements the same way ([SET-1]).

- [PY-COMPREHENSION-1] `[value for name in iterable if condition]` builds a fresh list through one checked loop over a list, string, byte string or dictionary, under the same fixed iteration bound and mutation check as `for`. An unfiltered comprehension reserves that bound before filling the list; a filtered comprehension retains ordinary growth so a sparse result does not reserve the full source size. The filter runs before the value, the binding lives only inside the comprehension, and the source collection is unchanged.
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-2] A one-clause, one-name list comprehension rejects a binding that shadows an active name, a non-Boolean filter, a source that is not a managed collection or an integer range, and a produced value that is not a copyable storable list element. A filter, two iteration names, several `for` clauses, dict comprehensions and generator expressions lower through their own loops instead ([PY-GATHER-1], [PY-GENERATOR-1]).
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-3] A comprehension accepts `range(...)` as its source exactly as a `for` loop does: `range(end)` and `range(start, end)` iterate a half-open ascending integer range whose bounds share one integer type, the binding takes each integer value, and the produced list has `int` elements when the body is the binding itself. The bounds evaluate once before the loop and an empty or reversed range yields an empty list. An unfiltered range comprehension and `list(range(...))` reserve the exact nonnegative range length with overflow-safe signed and unsigned arithmetic.
- [PY-RANGE-STEP-1] `range(start, stop, step)` counts from `start` by `step` in a `for` loop, a comprehension and `list(range(...))`: upward while below `stop` for a positive step, downward while above it for a negative one, and never past the end of the counter's integer type, so the last step cannot trap. All three bounds share one integer type. A literal zero step is `E_RANGE_STEP`, and a zero runtime step traps `R_ASSERT` with CPython's message `range() arg 3 must not be zero` before the first iteration.
- [PY-GATHER-1] A list or set comprehension with a filter, two iteration names or a second `for` clause, and every dict comprehension `{k: v for ...}`, lowers through ordinary loops: each `for` clause is the `for` statement it spells — so it accepts exactly what a `for` loop accepts, including `range` with a step, `enumerate`, `zip`, `reversed`, dictionary views and `for k, v in d.items()` — each `if` clause wraps what follows it — so a filter narrows the `Option` value it tests, as an `if` statement does — clauses nest left to right as Python reads them, and the innermost step appends, adds or writes one value. Every binding is confined to the comprehension. A dict comprehension evaluates the key before the value, and a repeated key keeps its first position and its last value; a set comprehension keeps each element's first position. An annotated destination supplies the element, key and value types to their literals. A one-clause, one-name list comprehension without a filter keeps its reserving form ([PY-COMPREHENSION-1]), and the same set comprehension its dedicated form ([SET-1]). A set comprehension's elements must be hashable ([HASH-KEYS-1]), as must a dict comprehension's keys.
- [PY-GENERATOR-1] `f(x for x in xs)` passes a generator expression as the sole argument, and `(x for x in xs)` is one anywhere a parenthesized argument is. `sum`, `any`, `all`, `min` and `max` fold its values through its own loops without building a list: `sum` starts from zero of the value's `int` or `float` type, `any` and `all` leave every loop of the generator at the first `True` or `False`, and `min`/`max` of no values trap `R_ASSERT` with CPython's `min() arg is an empty sequence`. `sorted`, `list` and `str.join` receive the list the loop builds, `set` the set, and `dict` the dictionary of its `(key, value)` tuples. A generator anywhere else — stored in a variable, passed to a declared function or beside a second positional argument — is `E_GENERATOR`, with the list comprehension as its alternative.
- [PY-GENERATOR-2] `len(x for x in xs)` is `E_GENERATOR`: a generator has no length, and the alternative names `sum(1 for x in xs if p)`. A generator argument written without its own parentheses beside another argument is `E_SYNTAX` at the comma, with the parenthesized spelling as its alternative.
- [PY-SET-OPS-1] On two integers `|`, `^` and `&` are bitwise or, xor and and (the `bit_or`, `bit_xor` and `bit_and` builtins), and on two sets the combining operators of [SET-OPERATORS-1]. They bind as Python binds them: `|` below `^` below `&`, all below `+` and `-` and above the comparisons, each left-associative, so `1 | 2 ^ 3 & 1` is `1 | (2 ^ (3 & 1))`. A `&` that follows an operand is the operator; a `&` where an operand begins is the removed argument marker, `E_REMOVED_SPELLING`.
- [PY-FSTRING-1] `f"..."` interpolates `{expression}` holes and constructs the pieces into one `str` with one exactly-sized result allocation rather than a chain of growing-prefix concatenations. A single piece returns its rendered text directly without a join or intermediate list. Holes evaluate and render exactly once from left to right. A hole takes any value `print` can render — lists, dictionaries, records, variants, results, options and byte strings as well as scalars — and renders it through the same renderer, so one value has one spelling wherever it is written. A hole holds one ordinary expression, including calls, operators, indexing and a nested format string, and is checked in its own source position. `f` before a quote is the prefix; anywhere else it is an ordinary identifier.
- [PY-FSTRING-2] `{{` and `}}` stand for single braces, ordinary string escapes apply to the literal pieces, and an empty format string is empty text. An empty hole, an unmatched or unterminated brace, more than one expression in a hole, a format specification its value cannot take (`PY-FSTRING-SPEC-2`), and a value with no rendering — a capability, an open file or a callback — are all rejected. Rendering releases its partial buffer on allocation failure and reports it as `OutOfMemory`, the way every other allocating writer in the compiler does.
- [PY-FSTRING-SPEC-1] `f"{value:spec}"` formats a `str`, any integer or a `float` under CPython's format-specification mini-language, `[[fill]align][sign][z][#][0][width][grouping][.precision][type]` with the types `d b o x X n e E f F g G %` and `s`, byte for byte as CPython 3.14 answers it: fill and `<`/`>`/`^`/`=` alignment by scalar count, `+`/space signs, `z`, `#` prefixes, sign-aware zero padding that extends `,`/`_` groups, and an integer under a float type converted as `float(value)` does. A float is rounded from its exact binary value with ties to even, so `f"{2.675:.2f}"` is `2.67`, `f"{0.125:.2f}"` is `0.12` and `f"{-0.001:.2f}"` is `-0.00`; with no type it is `repr`-style. An empty specification is the value's ordinary text.
- [PY-FSTRING-SPEC-2] A literal specification is checked when the program is: one the value's family cannot take (a sign or `#` on text, a precision on an integer type, `d` on a float, `,` with `x` or `n`, a missing precision, trailing characters) is `E_FORMAT_STRING` with CPython's reason, as is a `bool` or any value other than a `str`, an integer or a `float`, a width or precision above 4095, a nested `{...}` inside the specification, the `c` type and the fractional-digit grouping CPython 3.14 added.
- [PY-SCOPE-1] Locals are function-scoped, like Python: an assignment to a name anywhere in a function body refers to one binding that is in scope from its first assignment to the end of the body, and a nested block reassigns the same binding rather than introducing a new one. The binding's type is fixed at its first assignment and later assignments stay assignment-compatible. A `for` loop variable and a `match` payload binding remain scoped to their own block, and a move-only resource stays confined to the block that must discharge its cleanup.
- [PY-FOR-REBIND-1] A `for` loop variable is rebound from the loop at the top of every iteration, exactly as in Python, so assigning it in the body — `i = 10`, `i += 3`, a destructuring target `i, x = pair` — changes the variable for the rest of that iteration and never which iteration comes next or how many run. This holds for every loop form: `range(end)` and `range(start, end)`, both names of `enumerate`, the element of a list, string, bytes, dictionary, dictionary view or fixed array, `reversed`, both names of `zip` and of a two-value tuple or dictionary items, nested loops, and the same forms in a generic function body or a compile-time function's `range` loop. A counting loop — `range` or an `enumerate` position — whose body never assigns its variable counts on that variable itself, which is then immutable, so its lowering is unchanged and `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` may treat it as never negative; a body that can assign it (read from the syntax before the body is checked: an assignment, annotated binding or destructuring target naming it, or a place write rooted at it) gets its own mutable variable, copied from a hidden counter at the top of each iteration and not treated as non-negative. `continue` still advances the hidden counter; `break` still skips it.
- [PY-SCOPE-2] Reading a function-scoped local that no control-flow path reaching the read has assigned is rejected with `E_UNBOUND` naming where the binding is first assigned; a name never assigned before the read is still `E_UNKNOWN_NAME`. An assignment in every continuing branch of an `if`/`elif`/`else` binds the name afterwards, and an assignment in only some branches does not. Every `while`, ordinary `for`, `range`, `reversed` and `zip` loop may execute zero times, so assignments made only in the loop body do not bind the name after the loop; a name read after the loop must have been predeclared before it.
- [PY-SCOPE-3] Every `match` arm starts from the same pre-match definite-assignment state. A local is definitely assigned after the match only when every continuing arm assigned it; terminating arms contribute no continuation. A wildcard body counts as the continuation path for every variant it covers. Payload bindings remain scoped to their arm, while an ordinary local introduced in an arm remains function-scoped and must be assigned on every continuing path before it can be read after the match.
- [PY-STR-1] `str` values answer `strip`, `lstrip`, `rstrip`, `upper`, `lower` and `zfill(width)` with a fresh string, leaving the receiver and every alias unchanged. Case mapping covers ASCII letters and leaves every other scalar alone; `zfill` keeps a leading sign in front of the padding.
- [PY-STR-2] `replace(old, new)`, `find(sub)` (-1 when absent), `count(sub)`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `isdigit`, `isalpha` and `isspace` follow Python's answers, including `False` for every classification of empty text. Empty search/count needles follow PY-TEXT-RANGE-1; an empty split separator or replace needle traps `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`, and these methods are rejected on lists and dictionaries.
- [PY-STR-3] `splitlines()` splits on `\n`, `\r` and `\r\n` without keeping the terminators and yields no line for empty text. `int(text)` returns `Option[int]` and `float(text)` returns `Option[float]`, answering `None` rather than raising when the whole string is not exactly one number.
- [PY-STR-4] Every `str` method, scalar-value iteration and both number intrinsics lower to the QBE native backend over the immutable native text runtime, so no `str` operation reports `E_QBE_UNSUPPORTED`. A native `replace` with an empty needle raises `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`; empty `find` and `count` follow PY-TEXT-RANGE-1, and every method answers the value its documented semantics require.
- [PY-NUM-1] `int(text)` and `float(text)` accept one pinned base-10 grammar: optional surrounding ASCII whitespace, an optional `+` or `-` sign, then decimal ASCII digits separated by single `_` characters that must sit between two digits. `float` additionally accepts a `.` fraction, an `e`/`E` exponent with its own optional sign and digit run, and the case-insensitive spellings `inf`, `infinity` and `nan`. An alternate base such as `0x10`, a non-ASCII digit, an interior space, a trailing character and empty text are all `None`, and one shared vector table drives the checker and the native runtime so they cannot drift.
- [PY-NUM-3] A native program that parses numbers links the shared text runtime unit that holds the grammar, and `int(text)`/`float(text)` lower to an `Option` whose `Some` payload the runtime publishes and whose `None` carries no payload. A program that only measures or slices text links no such unit.
- [PY-NUM-2] `int(text)` answers `None` for a value the signed 64-bit range cannot hold rather than wrapping. `float(text)` is correctly rounded: a value beyond binary64 answers an infinity and one below it a signed zero, an absurd exponent saturates instead of wrapping, and digits past the rounding horizon are folded into a sticky digit that keeps the rounding the full text would have produced.
- [PY-ASSERT-1] `assert condition` checks a Boolean expression under any effect bound, in ordinary and generic function bodies, and traps `R_ASSERT` when it is false. The trap's source span is the checked expression and its default message is that expression's source text.
- [PY-ASSERT-2] `assert condition, "message"` reports the string literal instead of the expression text. Native lowering raises the same `R_ASSERT` code without the message, matching every other native trap.
- [PY-ASSERT-3] The message after assert may be any str expression, including an f-string; it is evaluated only when the condition is false and rendered to standard error immediately before the `R_ASSERT` trap, so a passing assertion pays nothing and a failed one names the values it saw. Because that expression may allocate, its effects count against the enclosing function's declared ceiling: a literal message stays free of effects, and an allocating message under `noalloc` or `effects()` is rejected.
- [PY-DIV-1] `divtrunc(a, b)` and `remtrunc(a, b)` are prelude helpers for integer division and remainder that round toward zero, the rounding `//` and `%` deliberately do not provide. They take two operands of one integer type, trap on a zero divisor and on the signed minimum divided by -1, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows them.
- [PY-CONVERSION-1] A `conversion` function body constructs its destination record as `T(a=v)`, under complete source-field accounting.
- [PY-VARIANT-3] A generic variant names only its template, because `Slot[T].some(v)` cannot be told apart from an index followed by a call. `Slot.some(v)` and the pattern `case Slot.some(v)` take their type arguments from the expected type — a binding annotation, parameter, field or result — in ordinary and generic function bodies alike, and report `E_GENERIC_INFERENCE` when no context supplies them. A project resolves the template through its own module names, so a declared or imported `m.Slot.some(v)` infers its arguments the same way.
- [PY-ARRAY-1] A list literal whose expected type is a fixed array `[N]T` — from an annotation, a parameter, a record field or a return type — constructs that array rather than a managed list, and a list literal in every other context still builds a managed list.
- [PY-ARRAY-2] Fixed-array literals check their length statically against the expected array, reporting `E_ARRAY_LENGTH`, and each element against the declared element type.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-1] A fixed array `[N]T` or enum table `[Kind]T` is written in place with the list spelling: `a[i] = v`, `a[i] op= v`, `a[i].f = v`, `a[i].f.g = v`, `a[i][j] = v` for an array of arrays, `r.cells[i] = v` through a value record, `c.cells[i] = v` through a `class`, and `xs[k][j] = v` or `xs[k].cells[j] = v` below a list or dictionary slot. The write is `a = replace(a, i, v)` for each array step and a record update for each field step, stored back into the binding the path starts at — or into the last list or dictionary slot on the path, which is a reference — so value semantics are those of `replace`: `b = a` copies, and a copy taken before the write keeps its value. The binding must be mutable (`E_IMMUTABLE` otherwise) and copyable. The assigned value is evaluated before the target's indices, an index is evaluated once even though it both reads and rebuilds the path, and a value that could write the binding (a call) is computed before the binding is read, so nothing it writes is lost. An index follows the fixed-array index rule of `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` exactly as a read and `replace` do: a negative index counts from the end, and an index outside `-N <= i < N` traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index as written and the length at the write's source position. A write whose path ends inside a `str` or `bytes`, or that is rooted anywhere other than a binding or a list or dictionary slot, keeps its existing diagnostic.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-2] Augmented assignment to a field below an array element, `a[i].f op= v` (and `r.cells[i].f op= v`, `xs[k][j].f op= v`), keeps its operator: the element's field is read once through the once-evaluated path, the operand is evaluated, the pair is combined with the operator's ordinary checked rules and the result is written back exactly as `a[i].f = combined` is, the way `record.field op= v` and `a[i] op= v` already are.
- [PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1] A fixed array `[N]T` takes Python's negative indices exactly as a list does: `a[-1]` is the last element and `a[-N]` the first, for a read, for every element write of `PY-ARRAY-WRITE-1` (`a[i] = v`, `a[i] op= v`, `a[i].f = v`, `a[i][j] = v`, a path below a record, `class` or list slot) and for `replace(a, i, v)`. An index outside `-N <= i < N` traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index as the program wrote it and the length, the code and detail a list reports; an index is still evaluated once. An enum table `[Kind]T` is indexed by its key and has no negative positions. The rule costs nothing where the checker can see the index is never negative — a non-negative constant, an unsigned value, a length, the induction variable of a `for` or comprehension over `range` from a non-negative start, an `enumerate` position the loop body never assigns, and a checked `+`, `*`, `//` or truncating remainder of such values, a `%` by a positive constant, a `&` with one, a right shift of one — and a constant in `-N .. -1` is folded to its position from the start; any other signed index is handled without a branch: one unsigned test of `i + N` against `2N` replaces the ordinary bounds test, and the element address is the ordinary `base + i * stride` plus `N * stride` selected by the sign of `i`.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-1] `for x in a`, `for i, x in enumerate(a)`, a two-name loop over an array of two-value tuples and `[f(x) for x in a]` walk a fixed array `[N]T` from index 0 to `N - 1`. A loop over an array place follows D1: later element writes are visible, and a named copy taken before the loop stays independent. A syntactic root assignment in the body and a non-place source select the entry value. The terminal needs no length guard because its length is fixed; indexed managed ancestors retain D1's exact length guards. An enum table `[Kind]T` is not walked by position and keeps `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. Generic bodies accept the same loops and comprehensions over `[N]T` with a `const N` length.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-2] A `for` loop over a fixed array held in a stable local or parameter reads elements directly from that variable, including after element writes or mut-callee writes, so walking the array copies nothing. A syntactic root assignment in the body retains an entry copy. A field or indexed array place is refreshed each step, with indices evaluated once; a non-place iterable retains its entry value.
- [PY-TABLE-1] A dict literal whose expected type is an enum-indexed table `[Kind]T` constructs that table, its keys spelled `Kind.case` and its values arranged in enum layout order independently of source order.
- [PY-TABLE-2] Table literals require exactly one entry per case of their own key enum; missing cases, duplicates, non-case keys and cases of a different enum are rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-2] Variant construction resolves through the declared type identity, so a local binding shadowing the type name keeps ordinary member resolution and a name that is not a declared case stays an ordinary call. Supplying no payload, more than one payload, a payload to an empty case, or a payload of the wrong type is rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-4] A union case may declare named fields as `case(f1: T1, f2: T2 = default)`, with the same default-literal and required-after-defaulted rules as record fields. `Type.case(v1, v2)`, `Type.case(f1=v1)`, and positional-then-named mixing construct it, omitting a defaulted field fills its literal; a `case: T` payload and a payload-free `case` keep their existing spelling and meaning. `case Type.case(p1, p2)` binds one name per named field (`_` discards that field), a single-payload case still takes one binding, and a payload-free case takes none. Arity, unknown-field, duplicate-field, missing-required-field, pattern-arity and dropped-result diagnostics are located. Exhaustiveness, wildcard arms, result obligations, equality, layout and native lowering behave exactly as for a single payload.


- [PY-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list literals infer homogeneous element types or use contextual list types; append, nested indexes, receiver expressions, and indexed writes evaluate operands once. Legacy writes retain receiver-first order; Python function writes evaluate the assigned value before the target. Signed negative indexes count from the end.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-2] List slices copy the selected elements into a fresh managed list, normalize negative endpoints and clamp to bounds. `len(list)` returns signed i64.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-3] For-each and enumerate evaluate a list once and retain its initial iteration bound, rejecting structural mutation through aliases before the next body or step; range accepts end or start/end. Break and continue retain correct step semantics.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-4] Contextual nested literals preserve list types across fields and nested indexes.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-5] Field assignment reconstructs mutable local records or list-slot records with receiver/index operands evaluated once, retaining value semantics for previous copies.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-6] Generic list literals, iteration, slicing and writes preserve symbolic element types and allocation effects.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-7] Receiver calls, computed indexes and assignment values run exactly once in source order and survive collection at every allocation.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-8] Structural list mutation through an entry snapshot traps during continued iteration, including continue and the final step; break exits without another iteration check. D1 live places allow growth and trap a decrease from the last observed length.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-9] Generic record field updates preserve managed alias copying and concrete value semantics.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-10] Slicing clamps negative and oversized endpoints, produces empty reversed intervals and copies list storage while retaining shallow element values.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-11] Contextual types attach to complete expressions so an annotated postfix expression does not incorrectly constrain its receiver literal.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-12] End-only range syntax starts at signed zero in runtime and compile-time scalar functions.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-13] Record field writeback retains unrelated fields changed through managed aliases during evaluation of the assigned value.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-14] `values[start:stop:step]` copies every `step`-th element of a list or `str`, applying Python's endpoint rules exactly once: an omitted endpoint defaults to the end the step walks from, endpoints are normalized against the length once, and a negative step walks backwards. The receiver, both endpoints and the step are each evaluated once, and a zero step traps with `R_SLICE_STEP`. Byte strings reject a step with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-15] `values.extend(other)`, `values.insert(index, value)`, `values.remove(value)`, `values.reverse()`, `values.sort()` and `values.clear()` mutate the list in place; `insert` counts a negative index from the end and clamps to the bounds, `remove` deletes the first equal element or traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`, and every method returns unit. `sort` stably orders integer, binary64, str and bytes elements by their scalar values in `O(n log n)` time with one reusable work buffer; NaNs follow ordered floats and equal values and signed zeros retain source order. `values.pop()` removes the last element and `values.pop(index)` removes the indexed element, both returning the element and trapping with `R_LIST_BOUNDS` when no element exists.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-16] `values.index(value)` returns the first equal element's index or traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`; `values.copy()` returns a fresh shallow list holding the same elements in the same order so later mutation of either list is not observed by the other.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-17] Dictionary `pop(key)` removes the matching entry and returns its value, `pop(key, default)` returns the default when absent, and `pop(key)` traps with `R_MISSING_KEY` when absent. `update(other)` writes every source entry into the receiver, replacing existing values while preserving their insertion position and appending new keys at the end; `setdefault(key, default)` returns the existing value or stores and returns the default. Insertion order remains the iteration order.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-18] `values += other` extends the receiver list in place, so every alias observes the added elements, and the assigned value must already have the receiver's list type. `d.items()` produces a live insertion-ordered view whose elements have `key` and `value` fields; iterating it with two names, `for key, value in d.items()`, unpacks those fields, while `list(d.items())` creates an independent shallow snapshot.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-19] `push` is removed; calling it reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` and names `append` as the replacement.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-20] Reading an absent list value (`index` or `remove`) traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`, popping an empty list traps with `R_LIST_BOUNDS`, and popping an absent dictionary key without a default traps with `R_MISSING_KEY`; no absent value is silently replaced by a sentinel.

- [PY-AUGASSIGN-1] `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `//=` and `%=` assign the operator's own result back to an existing binding, so `float` bindings take the float operators, `str` bindings concatenate, and integer bindings take the integer operators. `/=` on a non-float binding is rejected with `E_TYPE` because its result is a float, and a non-integer, non-float, non-`str` target is rejected with `E_TYPE` as before.

- [PY-AUGASSIGN-2] An augmented assignment writes through a list slot or dictionary entry: `values[index] op= value` and `counts[key] op= value` read the slot once, evaluate the assigned value first, apply the operator's rules to the element and write the result back through the same receiver and index, which are each evaluated once. The element type of `str` values concatenates and integer or `float` elements take their own operators.
- [AUGMENTED-FIELD-1] An augmented assignment writes through a field wherever plain assignment to that field is accepted: `counter.n += 1`, `h.inner.n -= 2`, `p.x *= 3` on a mutable record binding and `points[0].x += 5` evaluate the owner once, read the field, then evaluate the right-hand side, combine the pair with the operator's ordinary rules (integers, floats and `str` as for a binding, `/=` on an integer field is `E_TYPE`) and store the result, under the same mutability, `mut`-marker and arena rules as `target.field = value`. An overlapping evaluated `mut` argument is rejected by MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1; finish that call in its own statement before reading the current field. `+=` on a list field extends the current binding in place, as it does for a list binding, while named value snapshots remain independent.

- [CANONICAL-LEN-1] `len(value)` returns `int` for lists, dictionaries, strings, bytes and fixed arrays. The removed `.len()` member spelling reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at the member and names `len(value)` as its replacement.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-2] A user function declaration shadows a same-named prelude operation, including `len` and `replace`; its declared signature supplies the call arity and types. Module-local and imported callable references resolve those two spellings through the same visibility checks as other names, while unrelated modules retain their builtin operations.
- [PRELUDE-TYPES-2] The nominal catalog always registers `str`, `dict`, `bytes` and `float` unless a program declares that type name. Registration depends on parsed declarations and AST use, never on scanning raw source words or comment-like operator text.
- [PRELUDE-TYPES-CATALOG-1] Unused user nominal templates remain deferred while the concrete `str`, `bytes` and `float` prelude schemas are materialized unconditionally.
- [REMOVED-SPELLING-1] Removed operation, type, capability, codec, raw-float and punctuation spellings report a located `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` diagnostic naming the canonical replacement, or state that dead punctuation has no replacement.


- [PY-TEXT-1] Managed immutable text validates UTF-8, indexes Unicode scalar values (including negative indices), compares exact codepoints, concatenates, and splits/joins literal separators. Strings cannot be mutated through list operations. Scalar str conversions use Python Boolean/None spellings and numeric text. Python strings support Unicode escapes, codepoint slices, membership, and iteration; generic bodies preserve these types. Indexing an ASCII scalar returns a shared immortal one-character value, so a character-level scan does not allocate per index.
- [PY-TEXT-ORDER-1] `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` order two `str` values by Unicode code point and two `bytes` values by octet, lexicographically with a proper prefix first, exactly as Python does; mixing `str` and `bytes`, or ordering any other collection, is still `E_TEXT_OPERATOR`.
- [PY-REPEAT-1] `sequence * count` and `count * sequence` repeat a `str`, a `bytes` or a list by an integer of any width, as Python does: a count of zero or less answers an empty value, a repeated list holds an independent copy of each source element, as every list store does under value semantics, both operands are evaluated once in source order, and a repeated `str` or `bytes` is joined into one exactly-sized allocation. `*=` repeats a `str` or list binding in place of the binding.
- [PY-CHAIN-1] Comparisons chain as in Python: `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` share one precedence level, and `a < b <= c` means `a < b and b <= c` with each operand evaluated once, left to right, and every later operand skipped once a link is false. A parenthesized comparison is an ordinary `bool` operand, so `(a < b) < c` is not a chain.
- [STDLIB-TEXT-SCALAR-1] The `strings` module exposes `ord(text)` and `chr(code)`: `ord` returns the Unicode scalar value of a single-character text, `chr` returns that one-character text for a scalar value, both reject any other length, and `chr` rejects surrogates and code points past `0x10FFFF` with the module's `Invalid` or `Overflow` reason. Both are total `Result` values under the module's declared `managed_allocation` effect.
- [PY-DICT-1] Managed dictionaries preserve insertion order and independent values across assignments by default (shared alias updates only under `--legacy-sharing`), compare keys by value ([HASH-KEYS-1]), distinguish missing keys from defaults, trace key/value cycles, and fail allocation or capacity growth atomically. Managed data equality compares contents with cycle detection; dictionary equality ignores insertion order. Membership searches dictionary keys, list values, or a text substring. Python in/not in evaluate the needle before the container exactly once. Dictionary iteration returns keys in insertion order and traps if the iterated dictionary changes size; generic dictionary literals preserve symbolic key/value types.
- [DICT-VIEWS-1] Dictionary keys, values and items produce nonallocating ephemeral typed views for direct iteration, comprehensions and immediate len, membership, list, set and sorted consumption. Items yield DictItem key/value records and support two-name unpacking. Iteration observes value replacements and rejects dictionary size changes. list(d.keys()), list(d.values()) and list(d.items()) create independent snapshots in insertion order, including generic checking.
- [D2-VIEW-STORAGE-1] Builtin dictionary-view types are rejected at parameter, return, record-field and collection-element declarations, including unused generic declarations. A dictionary view expression cannot be bound, rebound, stored in any aggregate or collection, returned, discarded, projected through its mapping field or passed to a user function (including a mut argument). Concrete and symbolic generic checking reject these uses with E_VIEW_STORED. Certain snapshot migrations carry a structured list(...) fix around the exact keys, values or items expression; direct iteration and immediate len, in, list, set and sorted remain legal. User declarations that shadow builtin view names remain ordinary values.
- [LIVE-VIEW-ACYCLIC-1] The defensive internal catalog check rejects recursive storage cycles through compiler-constructed dictionary-view referents with E_LIVE_VIEW_CYCLE. Its iterative walk follows record fields, variant payloads, nonempty fixed arrays and collection keys/elements using builtin declaration identity. D2-VIEW-STORAGE-1 rejects source view annotations before layout checking, including phantom arguments and empty arrays; snapshots, ordinary recursive values and user declarations with view names remain valid.
- [PY-BYTES-1] `b"..."` and `b'...'` produce managed immutable `bytes` with the `[BYTE-LITERAL-1]` escapes and decoded-size limit; a literal keeps the fixed `[N]u8` array reading only where the context demands one, including readonly `&[]u8` slice parameters. `len(b)` is signed, `b[i]` yields an `int` in 0..255 with negative indices from the end and an `R_LIST_BOUNDS` trap outside them, `b[i:j]` copies a clamped byte range, `a + b` concatenates, `==`/`!=` compare content, `in`/`not in` search a byte scalar or a contiguous subsequence, and `for x in b` yields `int` under the list iteration-bound rules. Byte strings are immutable: no operation mutates a receiver, and in-place appends belong to the separate mutable `byte_buffer` of [PY-BYTES-6]. Byte strings are traced and rooted like `str`, and construction, concatenation, slicing and conversion contribute `managed_allocation`.
- [PY-BYTES-2] `bytes(values)` builds a byte string from a `list[int]` and traps with `R_BYTE_RANGE` outside 0..255; `b.to_list()` returns `list[int]`. `s.encode()` produces the UTF-8 bytes of a `str` and `b.decode()` returns `Result[str, str]` whose error reports invalid UTF-8 rather than raising. `b.hex()` formats lowercase hexadecimal text and `bytes.fromhex(s)` returns `Result[bytes, str]`, rejecting odd lengths and non-hexadecimal digits. `str(b)` is not provided.
- [PY-BYTES-21] `bytes.find_any` answers a one-octet set and a two-octet set with the libc bulk search instead of the general membership-table scan: one `memchr` for a single octet, and for two distinct octets two `memchr` passes from `start` whose earlier hit, if either, is the answer; a two-octet set whose octets repeat, an empty set, or a set of three or more octets keeps the bit-table scan. The answer is exactly the index the bit-table scan would report for every input, including a clamped or past-the-end `start`, a hit on either delimiter at the same index, and a high-octet set, so results and traps are unchanged.
- [PY-BYTES-22] A two-octet `bytes.find_any` searches a window at a time, the window doubling from 64 octets, and searches for the second octet only up to the first octet's hit inside the window, so a search stops within about twice the distance from `start` to its answer rather than scanning to the end of the input for an octet that is absent. A loop of searches that walks an input missing one of the two octets — `find_any(b"\n,", at)` over text with no commas — therefore costs time linear in the input instead of quadratic. The answer is exactly [PY-BYTES-21]'s for every input.
- [PY-BYTES-3] `b.split(sep)` returns `list[bytes]` and rejects an empty separator, `sep.join(parts)` rebuilds a byte string, and `b.startswith(x)`, `b.endswith(x)` and `b.find(x)` (returning -1 when absent) mirror the text operations. `b.find_any(values, start)` reads `values` as a set of octets and answers the first index at or after `start` holding any octet of that set, or -1 when none does: because `values` is a set, an empty one finds nothing and a repeated octet changes nothing; because this is a search and not an indexed read, a negative `start` clamps to 0 and a `start` at or past `len(b)` answers -1 rather than trapping. `bytes` is a prelude type usable in `list[bytes]`, `dict[str, bytes]`, record fields and `Option`/`Result` payloads; dictionary keys remain `int` or `str`. Like `str`, `bytes` is accepted in exported module signatures and rejected at JSON, host-function and native-embedding boundaries with those boundaries' existing diagnostics.
- [PY-BYTES-5] The QBE native backend lowers every byte-string operation, not only construction, `len`, indexing, iteration and equality: concatenation, clamped slicing, `in`/`not in` over a byte scalar or a subsequence, `split`/`join`, `startswith`/`endswith`/`find`, `find_any` as one `myl_native_bytes_find_any` call that performs the whole set search natively, `to_list`, `bytes(values)` with its `R_BYTE_RANGE` trap, `hex`/`fromhex` and `encode`/`decode` all run natively over a byte runtime that mirrors the managed text runtime with a one-byte stride, and report the traps and `Result` error text the language specifies.
- [PY-BYTES-6] `byte_buffer` is the mutable octet-buffer type beside the immutable `bytes`: the same one-octet elements, length, indexing, iteration, content search and content equality, but its own nominal type and a growable backing whose capacity exceeds its length. `byte_buffer(values)` builds one from a `list[int]` with the same `R_BYTE_RANGE` trap as `bytes(values)`, and only it declares the six in-place methods: `append_byte(value)` refuses a value outside 0..255, `append_range(data, start, end)` appends `data[start:end]` and refuses a negative `start`, an `end` below `start` or an `end` past `len(data)`, `append_utf8(text)` appends the UTF-8 encoding of a `str`, `append_int(value)` appends any `int`'s decimal spelling including the minimum signed value, `append_float(value)` appends the binary64's canonical spelling — byte for byte what `str(value)` prints, including the `.0` of an integral value and the `nan`/`inf`/`-inf` spellings — and `reserve(extra)` grows capacity for `extra` more octets while leaving `len` and every octet unchanged. Each answers a `bool` that is true exactly when it accepted the request, so a no-op success — `reserve(0)` or an empty range — answers true and writes nothing, while a refusal leaves the receiver byte-for-byte unchanged; each contributes `managed_allocation` and mutates only its receiver. An append writes the fragment's octets straight into spare capacity, growth doubles the backing with `O(log n)` allocations, and no append allocates a value for the fragment: `append_utf8` and `append_int` build neither a temporary `bytes` nor a temporary `str`, and both `append_int` and `append_float` write their spelling with no intermediate managed value at all — the float formatter builds the text on its own stack and appends it through the runtime's raw octet entry point. Length, indexing, iteration, comparison, search and every conversion read the buffer's current content, and identity is stable across growth, so aliases observe the same appends. Because the append methods belong only to `byte_buffer`, an immutable `bytes` literal, slice or view can never be written through, and a `byte_buffer` receiver is rejected with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` for every one of them. `bytes(buffer)` publishes the buffer's current octets as an immutable snapshot ([PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1]), so a later append cannot change an earlier snapshot; a comparison between the two byte kinds compares their octets as content.
- [PY-BYTES-7] `b.view(start, end)` answers a `bytes` that reads the range `b[start:end]` without copying it: the endpoints clamp exactly like the slice that would have copied them, so a negative start counts from the end, an end past the length clamps, and a reversed or empty interval is the empty byte string. The view retains the source's backing storage, so its octets stay readable after the byte string that first owned them is unreachable, and a view of a view reads the same block one range further in. Every byte-string read — length, indexing, iteration, comparison, membership, search, slicing, `decode`, `scalar_count`, numeric parsing — applies to a view unchanged, and slicing one copies the range it names. `b.copy()` materializes an owned copy that shares no storage with the receiver. Views are immutable: the in-place methods belong only to `byte_buffer`, so `view` and `copy` are rejected on a mutable buffer receiver with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` and no operation can write through a view. Constructing a view allocates its own small header and contributes `managed_allocation`; comparing, searching, counting, parsing and re-emitting a view allocate nothing.
- [PY-BYTES-8] `b.scalar_count()` answers `Result[int, str]` with the number of Unicode scalars in the receiver's octets, validated in the same nonallocating pass instead of decoding the range: ASCII runs advance a word at a time, and every other scalar is decoded with exactly the acceptance rules of the decoder, so an overlong encoding, a surrogate, a value above U+10FFFF, a truncated sequence and a stray continuation byte all answer `Err` carrying the decoder's invalid-UTF-8 reason, while the empty byte string answers `Ok(0)`. `str` has no such method because `len` already counts its scalars.
- [PY-BYTES-9] `b.parse_int()` and `b.parse_float()` answer `Option[int]` and `Option[float]` by reading the receiver's own octets through the one pinned number grammar, so a numeric leaf of a byte string or a view of one never decodes a temporary `str`: surrounding ASCII whitespace, the sign, the digit runs with their single `_` separators, the fraction, the exponent and the `inf`/`infinity`/`nan` spellings all behave exactly as they do for `int(text)` and `float(text)`, an overflowing integer answers `None` rather than wrapping, and a byte outside ASCII is rejected exactly where the scalar grammar rejects the non-ASCII scalar it encodes. Neither parse allocates on either representation, so `noalloc` accepts `int(text)`, `float(text)`, `bytes.parse_int()` and `bytes.parse_float()`, and a function whose only calls are these starts no collection and keeps no root protocol.
- [PY-BYTES-RANGE-1] `bytes.parse_int_range(start, end)`, `parse_float_range(start, end)` and `scalar_count_range(start, end)` inspect the same range as `view(start, end)` without allocating a view or copying its bytes. Endpoints accept negative offsets, clamp to the receiver, and make a reversed range empty. Number parsing retains the existing grammar and overflow behavior and is accepted under `noalloc`. Scalar counting returns `Result[int, str]`; its valid path allocates no managed value, but malformed UTF-8 may allocate the existing error string, so the method is rejected under `noalloc`.
- [PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1] Converting a `byte_buffer` to `bytes` — `bytes(buffer)`, and so `byte_builder.finish` — publishes the buffer's current octets as an immutable snapshot that reads them where they are: the conversion copies no octet and allocates only the snapshot's own small header, which retains the octets' block, so a snapshot stays readable after the buffer itself is unreachable. The buffer keeps its octets and its `len` and loses its spare capacity, so its next write first moves the octets into a private block sized the way the ordinary growth would size it; a published snapshot therefore never changes, a later append is visible only through the buffer and through snapshots taken after it, `bytes.copy()` still materializes an owned copy, and a buffer that is never written again pays no copy at all. The guarantees of [PY-BYTES-6] and [STANDARD-BYTE-BUILDER-1] — an earlier snapshot never changes, the builder stays usable, and an append of a snapshot back into its own builder works — are unchanged; only the mechanism that keeps them is.
- [PY-BYTES-FUSED-RANGE-1] An immutable byte slice or view consumed immediately by `parse_int()` or `parse_float()` lowers to the existing range parser before effect checking, so the canonical expression is accepted under `noalloc` and emits no slice copy or view header. Receiver and bounds run exactly once in source order, with omitted, negative, clamped and reversed bounds retaining ordinary semantics. A retained intermediate or a mutable `byte_buffer` slice is not fused.
- [UTF8-DECODE-1] The native bytes decoder validates UTF-8 in the same pass that counts and fills scalars instead of validating first and rebuilding second. ASCII input is one high-bit scan plus one widening pass; every other input runs the validating decoder once to count and once to fill, with no separate prior validation pass. It still rejects exactly the byte sequences the former validate-then-build pair rejected — overlong encodings, surrogate code points U+D800..U+DFFF, values above U+10FFFF, truncation and stray continuation bytes — publishes the same scalar sequence for accepted input, and still separates malformed input (`MYL_BYTES_INVALID`, reported as the `Result` error text) from a refused allocation (`MYL_BYTES_MEMORY`).

- [SCHEMATIC-PYTHON-1] The Python-syntax schematic uses GC collections and unit output helpers; decimal output preserves negative values including the minimum signed integer under Python floor division and modulo semantics.
- [PYTHON-CALL-1] Declared functions accept positional and keyword arguments with trailing scalar-literal defaults; argument effects run once in source order even when parameter order differs, including inferred generic calls.
- [PYTHON-CALL-2] Calls reject unknown, duplicate, missing, excess, and misplaced arguments. Defaults require immutable scalar literals, their declared types are checked even in unused functions, and required parameters cannot follow defaults.
- [PYTHON-CALL-3] Python constructor calls resolve records by declaration identity, support positional and named fields, generic records, and empty `class` declarations using `pass`.
- [PYTHON-CALL-4] Keyword/default binding, source-order lowering, execution, and native emission release partial allocations on allocator failure.
- [PYTHON-CALL-5] Editing a default value invalidates checked caller reuse and produces fresh call behavior.

- [C-FFI-1] Explicit `extern def` declarations use fixed-arity C scalar signatures on QBE: signed/unsigned 8/16/32/64-bit integers, bool, float as C double, and unit results as C void. They require effects(host), bypass provider registration, retain transitive external effects, and reject borrowed/aggregate/generic signatures. Trusted host-provider registration rejects direct imports. Declaration listing and module discovery treat `extern` as a declaration head, so a newline-terminated declaration ends where the next one begins.
- [HOST-CAPABILITY-1] Every `extern def` and `host def` declares `host: Host` as its first parameter. The checker requires a `Host` value at every call and the C ABI lowering drops that operand before the foreign call. `effects(host)` stays inferred and transitive so effect ceilings keep working. Under `--restrict`, `--allow-host` grants the authority; it is granted by default like the other capabilities. A callback type whose parameters include `Host` is the only function-value route to C.
- [QBE-INTOPS-2] A checked 64-bit signed add or sub whose right operand is a literal is tested against the single operand value that can carry it out of range (`max - c` or `min + c`) instead of the general sign test, so `x + 1` costs one comparison rather than three; the literal is used only when it is the operand's linear producer and no instruction can jump directly to the operation, and every other case keeps the general test, the same trap code, the same operand detail, and the same result bits.
- [QBE-FRAME-ACCOUNTING-1] Call-depth accounting follows instruction-budget accounting: a native build that was not given a frame ceiling emits neither the depth counter nor the `--max-frames` ceiling test (the execution-stack floor guard and the frame record that trap traces are built from both stay), and its entry point refuses a runtime `--max-frames` with `R_NO_FRAME_ACCOUNTING` instead of ignoring it. A build that was given a ceiling, and every `mylang run`, keeps the exact per-call count.
- [QBE-FRAME-ACCOUNTING-2] Call depth follows the same rule as instruction budgets: `mylang run --max-frames=N` and `mylang test` compile a per-call count in, an executable `mylang build` publishes carries none, and such an executable given `--max-frames=` in `MYLANG_OPTIONS` refuses it before `main` runs with `R_NO_FRAME_ACCOUNTING` ("run it under `mylang run --max-frames=N`") and exit status 1, after RUN-FRAME-BUDGET-1's reservation check.
- [QBE-FLOOR-GUARD-1] A function that transfers control to no other guest frame skips the prologue's execution-stack floor test: its own frame is bounded at compile time and its caller already proved the frame pointer sits a whole 64 KiB headroom above the guard page, so a leaf that stays well inside that headroom cannot reach the page. Functions that call anything keep the guard, and every other prologue behaviour, trap code and trace is unchanged.
- [LINT-7] `untested-export` reads the shared tests-reachability index instead of only the direct callees of discovered tests: under the `agent` profile an export that any discovered test reaches, directly or transitively, is tested and produces no finding, while an export no test reaches is reported as not reached; under `strict` and `safety` the direct-call fact is kept, an export only reached transitively is reported with the wording "reached only transitively", and an export no test reaches keeps the "not called directly" wording. The rule and the related notes on `mylang lint` findings read the one index `mylang tests-for` answers from, built once per run, so the three cannot disagree.
- [FRICTION-LOG-1] Friction logging is opt-in and its destination is decided in one place: `--friction-log=PATH` outranks the `MYLANG_FRICTION_LOG` environment variable, an absent or empty destination means off, and a command that was not asked for a log writes nothing, creates no log file, and never changes its exit status because a log could not be written.
- [FRICTION-LOG-2] When logging is enabled the compiler appends exactly one `friction-log-v1` JSON line per command, carrying only the compiler's build identity, the command spelling, the source path exactly as the user typed it, the SHA-256 of the checked source snapshot, each rendered diagnostic's code, severity and byte span, the command's exit status, and its monotonic wall time in nanoseconds; no other field and no diagnostic message text is written.
- [FRICTION-LOG-3] A friction record never contains source text, diagnostic message text, environment values, or an absolute path the user did not pass, and the log destination itself is not recorded; a session logged over a source file whose text and diagnostic message both quote a sentinel string leaves the sentinel absent from the log.
- [PARSER-HINT-1] A `union`, `enum` or `result` body whose next case sits on its own line at the case column without its separating comma reports `E_SYNTAX` at that case with the declaration's own noun — `union cases are separated by commas`, `enum cases are separated by commas`, `result cases are separated by commas` — and an `alternative` note `insert a comma after the previous case` located at the previous case. The code and the offending span are the ones the former `expected a dedent` failure reported; a token that is not an identifier at the case column keeps that former message, so this is targeted messaging at one known shape rather than error recovery, and the parser still stops at its first error.
- [PARSER-HINT-2] A bare module-level assignment (`MAX = 42`) and its annotated spelling (`MAX: int = 42`) report `E_SYNTAX` at the assigned name with the `alternative` note `declare it as a constant: const MAX: int = 42`, instead of the bare `expected def` the function head produced.
- [PARSER-HINT-3] A `def` inside a `record` or `class` body — whether it is the first body entry or follows declared fields — reports `E_SYNTAX` at the `def` with the `alternative` note `declare a top-level function taking the record: def norm1(p: Point) -> int`, instead of `expected a name`.
- [PARSER-HINT-4] A top-level `from module import name` reports `E_SYNTAX` at `from` with the `alternative` note `import the module and declare a record: import dataclasses`, instead of the bare `expected def`, so the rejection names both accepted forms before the decorator line is reached.
- [PARSER-HINT-5] The hinted declaration forms are one table: `declaration_hints` in `src/parser.zig` names the same idioms in the same order as the `hints` array in `velocity/friction/classifier.json`, each with an `E_SYNTAX` rule behind it, so the idiom suite's classification and the parser's messaging cannot drift. A unit test fails when either side gains a form the other lacks, and fails — rather than skips — when the classifier cannot be read.
