- [MUT-PARAM-8] A missing or superfluous call-site `mut` marker does not end its function's check: the checker records the `E_MUTATION_MARKER` diagnostic with its fix, checks the call as if the marker were right, and fails the function once its body is done, so one `check` reports every marker a function needs rather than one per run. A `mut` on an argument that cannot be assigned through still ends the function's check at once.
- [MUT-PARAM-2] On the declaration side the same error covers both directions. A parameter the body writes with no `mut` is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` at the parameter, whose message lists each written parameter by name and position and gives the exact declaration to paste (`values: mut list[i64]`); a parameter declared `mut` that the body never writes is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` naming the parameter and `remove `mut``. Forwarding a parameter as `mut` to another call counts as a write, so a wrapper that only forwards is correctly `mut`, and a parameter whose only writes are provably fresh local storage is not a write at all. Under value semantics, writes through a `match` payload binding are `E_VALUE_BINDING_WRITE`; copy it to a writable local and store the modified value back. Compatibility-mode payload and narrowed-Option aliases instead retain the matched owner's mutation origin.
- [MUT-PARAM-3] `mut` is accepted on mutable collections, fixed arrays and records, including scalar-only records under value semantics. A scalar, `str` or `bytes` parameter rejects with `E_MUTATION_MARKER` and a hint to return the new value instead. A `mut` argument must be assignable: a variable, field or indexed slot rooted at one, so `f(mut [1, 2])` rejects with a message to assign the value to a local first. Compatibility mode additionally permits `class` and builtin `Cell`, and keeps its managed-bearing record restriction.
- [PY-ARRAY-MUT-1] `a: mut [N]T` (and `mut [Kind]T`) is accepted for every element type that is copyable without a pending result, and makes the parameter name the caller's own array for the whole call: `a[i] = v` in the callee writes the caller's variable, and so does passing `mut a` on to another such parameter. The caller writes `f(mut a)` under the ordinary marker rules, and the argument must be a mutable variable — a local array or a `mut` array parameter being passed on; a field or element is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` saying to copy it into a local, pass that and store it back. An element write through an array parameter that is not `mut`, directly or by passing it as `mut`, is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` naming the parameter, exactly as a list parameter's write is, and a `mut` array parameter the body never writes is the same unused-`mut` error. Rebinding a `mut` array parameter as a whole (`a = other`, `a = replace(a, i, v)`, a destructuring target) is `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`, because the name is the caller's array. `f(mut a, a)` gives the read-only parameter a snapshot taken at the call, and `f(mut a, mut a)` is `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` (MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1). A function with a `mut` array parameter cannot be a function value (`E_CALLBACK_TYPE`) and a lambda cannot fill a callback with one (`E_LAMBDA_CONTEXT`), and a host or C ABI function cannot declare one. Whole reassignment of an ordinary array parameter, which never reaches the caller, is unchanged.
- [MUT-PARAM-4] A `mut` parameter contributes `mutation` to the declared-ceiling check; exceeding an effect ceiling is `E_MUTATION_PERMISSION`. Forwarding a caller parameter as `mut` requires that parameter to be declared `mut`. With default value semantics, a read-only argument overlapping a writable argument receives a snapshot. Under `--legacy-sharing`, a declared `mut` footprint rejects writable/read-only arguments that may alias with `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP`, including two names for the same fresh local, while a separate copy is disjoint.
- [MUT-OVERLAP-PROOF-1] The disjointness proof behind `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` is a reachability search over unordered pairs of types: starting from the writable and the read-only argument types, a pair is replaced by either side's managed components (list and dictionary elements, array elements, domain backings, union payloads and fields), and the arguments may alias exactly when some reachable pair is one type, one nominal record, or a record the catalog cannot describe. Each pair is expanded at most once per query, so the cost is polynomial in the number of reachable types: two families of mutually recursive classes check at once where the per-path walk it replaces never finished, and a shared component anywhere in either graph still rejects the call.
- [MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1] An assignment or update receiver may not overlap a `mut` argument evaluated in that same statement: the same root and paths that may agree until either ends give E_MUTATION_OVERLAP at the mut argument with the receiver as related evidence. Make the call in its own statement first and use its saved result. Disjoint roots and paths, a call in a preceding statement, and read-only snapshots remain legal. A simple unconditional one-line update with no other effectful evaluation carries the exact structured split-call fix; conditional or effectful rewrites require the programmer to choose their ordering.
- [ORIGIN-WIDE-1] The checker's mutation and alias analysis names every parameter position of a function exactly, so a `mut` collection, a returned read-only collection or a returned string at any parameter position checks, builds and runs (a 17th such parameter used to panic the checker). A list, string, record or other aggregate parameter must be one of a function's first 64 parameters (`E_PARAMETER_POSITION` at a later one, advising to move it earlier or group parameters into a record); scalar parameters are never tracked and stay unlimited. An unattributed write publishes every parameter position as possibly written.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1] At one call the places named by any two `mut` arguments must be disjoint, or the call is `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP`. A `mut` argument names a place: a root variable plus a path of steps, each a field name or a list, fixed-array or dictionary index. Two places overlap when they have the same root and every pair of steps compared in order may be equal until one path ends, so a variable overlaps each of its parts and a place overlaps itself. Two field steps are equal exactly when they name the same field. Two index or key steps are provably distinct only when both are compile-time constants (an integer or bool literal or a scalar `const`, a negated integer literal, a string or bytes literal, a payload-free enum case; arithmetic such as `1 + 1` is not folded) that differ; a negative list or array index counts back from the end, so `xs[-1]` and `xs[2]` may name one element while two different negative constants, or two different non-negative ones, are distinct, and two different constant dictionary keys are distinct whatever their signs. Anything else may be equal. So `f(mut b, mut b)`, `f(mut a, mut a[0])`, `f(mut r, mut r.rows)`, `f(mut xs[i], mut xs[j])`, `f(mut xs[0], mut xs[0])` and `f(mut xs[-1], mut xs[2])` are rejected, while `f(mut s.a, mut s.b)`, `f(mut xs[0], mut xs[2])`, `f(mut rows[i].a, mut rows[j].b)`, `f(mut d["a"], mut d["b"])` and `f(mut rt, mut o)` stay legal. The rule makes two `mut` parameters of one call disjoint, which the copy analysis and the RC optimizer assume.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-PROOFS-1] Under value semantics, accepted mutable arguments are disjoint, so another mutable parameter alone does not prevent readonly interval or operand anchors, plain-record move aliases, record field return or mut-list element field return. Legacy sharing uses collector rooting and retains the other conservative alias refusals; observations, overlapping argument diagnostics, pending references, writes, exits and surviving temporaries keep their existing checks.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-2] MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 applies to every call that takes `mut` arguments: direct and module-qualified calls, keyword and mixed arguments (compared after binding to parameters, so `f(second=mut xs[0], first=mut xs[0])` is rejected and `f(second=mut xs[2], first=mut xs[0])` is not), and calls through function values and callbacks; builtins take no `mut` arguments. It compares `mut` arguments only: a read-only argument next to a `mut` one keeps the VALUE-SEMANTICS-5 snapshot (`touch(mut a, a)`), and a `mut` call nested inside another argument (`emit(mut p, make_constant(mut p, c))`) finishes before the outer call begins and stays legal. The rule holds under default value semantics and under `--legacy-sharing` alike, so the migration mode keeps no hole the optimizer could fall into.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-3] The MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 diagnostic points at the later of the two arguments in source order and carries the earlier one as its related span. Its message names the callee and says why the places overlap — both name one place, one is part of the other, two indices or keys that are not two different constants, or a negative index against a non-negative one — then gives the rewrite: copy one into a local, pass `mut` that local and store it back (`copy = a[0]`, pass `mut copy`, then `a[0] = copy`), or pass the enclosing value (`mut a`) once.
- [MUT-TRACK-1] Mutation tracking follows writes that reach a `mut` parameter, and its missing-marker diagnostic points at the first write to an unmarked parameter. Under value semantics, ordinary local copies are independent and writes through `match` payload bindings reject with `E_VALUE_BINDING_WRITE`. Under `--legacy-sharing`, writable payload bindings and their local aliases retain the matched owner's origin; binding origins reach a fixed point across loops, including an origin widened by a later statement.
- [MUT-PARAM-5] With `mut` supplying the footprint, the `mutates(...)` clause and the opt-in `&` call-argument marker are removed spellings: a clause is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at `mutates` saying “mark each written parameter `name: mut T`”, `&` before an operand is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` saying to write `mut value` (between two operands it is the set intersection, [SET-OPERATORS-1]), and the `--mutation-markers` switch is rejected on every command. The reference-typed `class` exemption and the advisory `W_MUTATES_MISSING` warning are gone with them: a compatibility-mode write through a class parameter needs its own `mut` like every other position, and the check is an error, not a warning.
- [MUT-PARAM-6] Every signature surface renders the new spelling: `list-interfaces`, `context --symbol`, `schema-project` and `api-diff` print `name: mut T` in the signature text (so `interface_sha256` covers it), the separate `mutates(...)` column is dropped from the interface listing while the published `mutates` field name and its position list stay for consumers, and `mylang fmt` returns `name: mut T` and `mut argument` unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A parameter declared after a bare `*` binds by name only: a call that supplies one positionally is `E_KEYWORD_ONLY`, reported at the callee, naming that parameter and printing the whole call rewritten with every keyword-only operand passed by name (`io.issue(host, code=13, file=0, position=span.start)`). The repair is rendered from the call site's own source text by the one call renderer the call-site rejections use, so callee, operand order and operand spelling are the ones written. A required keyword-only parameter that no operand supplies keeps the ordinary `E_ARITY`, and binding by name in any order, mixed with positional operands, is unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-2] The keyword-only boundary is part of the declaration, not of a call: required and defaulted keyword-only parameters may mix in any order, an omitted defaulted keyword-only parameter takes its declared default, and the positional group keeps the required-before-defaulted rule. `keyword_only_from` reaches binding through the declared `FunctionSignature`, so every call site that binds a declared function — an ordinary call, a compile-time `const` call and a generic body — enforces the same rule.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-3] The rendered interface signature carries the `*`: `list-interfaces` prints `def(host:Host,*,code:int)->i64` for a declaration that makes `code` keyword-only, so the published contract and the call-site rejection agree on which parameters are positional.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-1] `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` is an expression whose condition must be `bool` and whose two arms have one type: the contextual expected type when the destination supplies one, and otherwise the then arm's type, with an integer literal arm adopting the expected width and no numeric widening between arms. Arms that disagree are `E_TYPE` `conditional arms must agree: \`int\` here, \`str\` in the else arm`, reported at the first arm; a non-`bool` condition is the ordinary `E_TYPE` `expected bool, found int`.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-2] A conditional is one expression wherever an expression is accepted: a `return` value, an assignment or binding value, a call or keyword-argument value, a list or record-constructor element, an f-string hole, and the else arm of another conditional (which is why `a if p else b if q else c` nests to the right). `Some(x) if p else None` and `Ok(x) if p else Err(e)` take their arm type from the expected `Option` or `Result`, exactly as those constructors do in any other contextual position.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-3] Only the chosen arm is evaluated, so `a if p else f()` never calls `f` when `p` holds and an unchosen arm that would trap does not. Both arms are still checked: each contributes its reads (including `E_UNBOUND` for a name no path reaching it assigns), its effects and its origins, and a standalone conditional whose arms are not unit keeps `E_EXPLICIT_DISCARD_REQUIRED` like any other non-unit expression statement.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-4] The check lowers the conditional to the if/else the rest of the compiler already understands — a fresh unnamed temporary that each arm assigns and the block reads — so no new checked node, effect rule, origin rule or bytecode shape is introduced, and a conditional inside a generic function body is checked symbolically with the same arm agreement and condition rules.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-4] The `positional-literal` lint (report-only, `L_POSITIONAL_LITERAL`) reports the last operand a call of three or more arguments passes positionally as a `bool`, integer or `None` literal, at the callee: `` `True` is passed positionally to `draw`'s 4th parameter `filled`; name it: `draw(canvas, x=10, y=20, filled=True)` ``. The reported parameter is the one that operand binds to, an intrinsic or a call through a callback value is exempt because it has no parameter to name, and the repaired call comes from the shared call renderer with every operand from the first positional literal on written by name, so the paste is a call that binds.
- [TUPLE-1] A tuple is an anonymous record interned in the record catalog, keyed by its element types in source order, with positional fields named `$0`, `$1`, … that no source can spell; a module program keeps every `tuple[...]` spelling through the merge that combines its units, so a tuple type is one identity in a single file and in a project alike; the element list always interns to one identity, whether it is reached from a `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` spelling through the layout graph or from a value through `record_layouts.tupleRecord`, so `tuple[int, str]` is one type in signatures, comparisons, caches and interface rendering. A spelling outside the two-to-eight element range is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` at the whole `tuple[...]` spelling, and a tuple is a plain record everywhere else: construction is a record construct, a read is a positional field read, and the contract of the whole (storable, move-only, result-handling, managed-bearing) is the element-wise rule `record_layouts` already derives from fields.
- [TUPLE-2] `(a, b)` and the bare forms after `return` build a tuple value from elements checked in source order, with the expected-type rule for literals: when the destination already demands `tuple[T1, T2]` each element is checked against its positional type, and otherwise the element types are inferred and interned. A tuple may be stored in a list, as a dictionary value, in a record field, in an `Option` or `Result` payload, and nested in another tuple; it is managed-bearing exactly when one of its elements is, and a value whose arity differs from the demanded tuple type is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the count it has and the count it needs.
- [TUPLE-3] `x, y = value` destructures a tuple: the value is evaluated once into one temporary, its arity is checked against the number of names (`E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the source of the value and the number of names to bind), and each name is bound to the positional field of that temporary, so `a, b = b, a` swaps with no intermediate list string. `_` in a target list binds nothing and reads nothing, a name may be rebound by the assignment like any other assignment, and a right-hand side that is not a tuple is `E_TYPE` naming the single-binding alternative.
- [PY-DESTRUCTURE-PLACE-1] A destructuring target list may name elements and fields as well as names: `a[i], a[j] = a[j], a[i]` swaps two elements of a fixed array or list, and `d[k], r.f, xs[-1].g, name = value` writes a dictionary entry, a record field, a field of an element and a binding. The value is evaluated once, before any target, into one temporary whose arity is checked as `TUPLE-3` says; then each target in order evaluates its own receiver and indices and takes its element exactly as `target = element` would, with the same type, mutability, `mut`-parameter, arena and negative-index rules. A target that is not a name, element or field is `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`.
- [TUPLE-4] `for number, letter in pairs` destructures each two-element tuple element through the same two-binding path `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` already use, so the loop variable names stay scoped to the body and the element type is the tuple's two positional types; a tuple element of any other arity is `E_TUPLE_ARITY` naming the one-name alternative.
- [TUPLE-5] A tuple has no elements to index, no fields, no methods and no `len`: `pair[0]` is `E_TUPLE_INDEX` naming the destructuring alternative, `pair.head` and `pair.count()` are `E_TYPE` naming the same alternative, and `len(pair)` is `E_TYPE` naming it too. A tuple type compared against a different tuple type is `E_TYPE` through the ordinary expected/found message, dictionary keys stay `int` and `str` so a tuple is never a key, `DictItem`, `zip` and `enumerate` are unchanged, `match` patterns over tuples are not accepted, and the host and JSON boundary rejects a tuple graph like any other non-data graph.
- [TUPLE-6] A tuple prints as `(1, 'a')` — parenthesized, comma-space separated, each element rendered exactly as `print` renders that element alone, so a text element is repr-quoted — and `==` on two tuples follows the aggregate rule: structural, element-wise and recursive, comparing text content rather than reference.
- [TUPLE-7] A tuple type renders in its source spelling, `tuple[int, str]`: `mylang fmt` returns a tuple value `(a, b)`, a `tuple[...]` type and a destructuring target list unchanged, and `list-interfaces` renders a signature that names a tuple through the canonical catalog name `tuple<i64,str>`, which the same surface rewriter turns back into `tuple[int, str]`.
- [TUPLE-8] A tuple type in a template keeps its elements abstract: `tuple[T, int]` is the ordered argument list of the anonymous tuple declaration, not a representative concrete type, so a generic function may take, return and destructure such a tuple and the bound element keeps its parameter identity. Requirements on a template tuple are its elements' requirements rather than a declaration-level guarantee, and a fully concrete element list materializes the same interned record that a closed `tuple[...]` spelling does, so a generic body and its specializations agree on identity.
- [OPTION-METHODS-1] `Option[T]` answers three intrinsic methods and nothing else: `unwrap_or(default: T) -> T`, `is_none() -> bool` and `is_some() -> bool`. They are reachable on both receiver spellings — a named local, whose call is checked where the collection receiver arm runs, and any other expression, whose method call is checked on the `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` dispatch path — and `unwrap_or`'s default is checked against the payload type with the ordinary expected-type rule, so `head(narrow).unwrap_or(7)` adopts a `u8` payload and a mismatched default reports `E_TYPE` with `` `unwrap_or` default must be `int` (the Option's payload), found str ``. All three are pure, allocate nothing, and lower through `option_flow.zig`'s existing exhaustive variant dispatch: presence tests to the tag test and `unwrap_or` to a two-arm match that roots the receiver, evaluates the default eagerly into a fresh temporary and binds either the payload or that temporary into one result slot.
- [OPTION-METHODS-2] A generic body accepts the same three methods through the symbolic checker, so a template whose parameter is an `Option[T]` may call `unwrap_or`, `is_none` and `is_some` without an instantiation: the default is checked against the symbolic payload and the method contributes no effect, so a `noalloc` ceiling accepts every one of them. An `Option` carrying an owned value or a checked result still requires explicit `match`, exactly as a presence test does, and a receiver that has no such method reports `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` naming `unwrap_or(default)`, `is_none()` and `is_some()` rather than the collection vocabulary.
- [OPTION-METHODS-3] The `sentinel-int` lint reports an `int` used as a sentinel for the absent case: a function whose declared result is an integer and which returns a negative integer literal on some path, where a local bound from that call (or the call itself) is compared against a negative literal — or the `< 0`/`>= 0` presence guards — is reported at that comparison with `` `find_pin` returns -1 on one path and its result is tested against a negative literal here; return `Option[int]` and test `is None` or use `unwrap_or` ``, and a record field or a parameter compared against `-1` in two or more places is reported once with the same rewrite. The rule is `L_SENTINEL_INT`, report-only under `agent` and blocking under `strict` and `safety`; it is exempt by name for `find` and `find_any`, whose `-1` is their own contract.
- [MUT-PARAM-7] A `mut` argument marker inside a generic function body is transparent to the symbolic checker: the marked value is checked with the type it would have unmarked, so a template that forwards `mut` to a declared `mut` parameter checks and its concrete specializations re-check every call with the declared footprint and full origin analysis. The marker therefore has one spelling and one meaning in an ordinary body and in a template alike.

- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-1] A function's effect ceiling is derived from its parameter list: a `Stream` parameter permits `output`, `Filesystem` `filesystem`, `Network` `network`, `Clock` `clock`, `Entropy` `random`, `Environment` `environment`, `Host` `host`, a `mut` parameter `mutation`, a callback parameter the bounded ceiling its type declares, and `managed_allocation` is always permitted; the ownership a parameter or the result type carries contributes the effect of the resource family it holds, because a value the body may drop is authority the signature already grants. A body that exceeds that ceiling is rejected in that body with `E_EFFECT_BOUND` at the operation or call that reaches the effect, and the message names the parameter to add (`add `out: Stream` to the parameters`). A `host def` or `extern def` has no body to derive from, so its clause stays its ceiling as written, and a callback type whose ceiling is omitted is unbounded rather than a promise: it contributes nothing to the derived set and an invoke through such a value contributes nothing either, because the value's own signature is where that authority is declared.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-2] `noalloc`, `noio` and `effects(...)` survive only as narrowings of the derived ceiling. A clause that permits exactly what the parameters imply — `effects(host, managed_allocation)` on a `Host` parameter, or `noio` on a body with no io-capable parameter — is `W_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, whose message quotes the clause and says to remove it; `noalloc` never restates, because allocation is always permitted. The warning blocks under `lint --profile=strict`, where it is the `derived-effects` rule.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-3] A clause that names an effect no parameter can supply is `E_DERIVED_EFFECTS` at the clause, because it can never be satisfied: the message lists every unsupplied effect and the parameter paste that would supply each one (`add `fs: Filesystem` to the parameters or drop the clause`). Only an explicit `effects(...)` list can name one; `noalloc` and `noio` withhold effects instead and are checked only for restating the derived set.
- [DERIVED-EFFECTS-4] The published `ceiling` is the derived set narrowed by an explicit clause, for a declaration whose body was never checked as much as for a checked one: `list-interfaces` prints `ceiling(host,managed_allocation)` from the parameter list alone beside `analysis(declaration-only)`, the contract manifest's `permissions` and the public schema publish it, and `interface_sha256` carries it in place of the inferred mask. The published `effects` fact stays the checked body fact, so a body-only edit that adds an allocation inside the derived ceiling changes `body_sha256` and leaves every declaration interface and caller type check untouched.
- [UNUSED-CAPABILITY-1] A capability parameter the body never reads is `E_UNUSED_CAPABILITY` at the parameter, replacing the `W_UNUSED_BINDING` it used to be: the message names the parameter and says the signature claims an effect the body cannot have (`remove the parameter or use it`). Reading it at all counts as use, including handing it to another call, so a frame that only forwards its `Host` keeps it; `Stream` and `Host` are covered like every other capability, and a `host def`/`extern def` declaration, which has no body, is exempt.

## Self-hosting oracle

- [SELFHOST-LEXER-DUMP-1] `mylang dump-tokens <file.my>` lexes exactly one file and writes a single-line `tokens-v1` JSON document to stdout with the compiler's own token stream: a `version` string, the `path`, a `count`, and one record per token in source order carrying the exact `@tagName` kind spelling, the half-open byte `start`/`end`, and 1-based `line`/`column`. It stops at the lexer — it never loads imports, parses, checks or lowers — and it is the stage-0 reference a differential self-host lexer is compared against. A lexical failure prints the ordinary diagnostics to stderr, writes no partial document, and exits 1.

## Contracts

- [CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1] `@requires(cond[, "message"])` and `@ensures(cond[, "message"])` attach to a `def` with a body — top-level, a domain member (public or `private`), generic or not — and `@invariant(cond[, "message"])` attaches to a non-generic `record` or `domain`. Each is repeatable and checked in source order; the message is a plain string literal. The clause on any other declaration (`class`, `union`, `enum`, `result`, a type alias, a constraint, a `const`, an `extern` or `host` def, a generic record) is `E_CONTRACT_TARGET`, and inside a domain body only contract clauses attach to a member (`E_ATTRIBUTE_TARGET`). A condition that is not `bool` is `E_TYPE`. The condition is part of the parsed declaration: the project merge relocates it with its module and the persisted frontend syntax cache stores it.
- [CONTRACT-REQUIRES-1] A `@requires` condition is checked at function entry, after the parameters are bound and before the body, in a scope holding only the parameters (plus module constants and functions). A false one traps `R_REQUIRES` located at the clause; the report names the function, the clause text and its message, says `precondition violated`, and names the caller that broke it — the next frame out — as `broken by the caller NAME at PATH:LINE:COLUMN`.
- [CONTRACT-ENSURES-1] An `@ensures` condition is checked at every normal return, including the implicit end of a procedure, in a scope holding only the parameters (a parameter's value at exit, so a `mut` parameter shows the body's writes), `result` (the returned value) and `old(...)`. A function returning `Result[T, E]` checks it only on an `Ok` return, with `result` the `T` payload; an `Err` return and a `?` that propagates one skip it. A false one traps `R_ENSURES` located at the clause. In a function with `@ensures`, `result` names the returned value, so a parameter named `result` is `E_CONTRACT_RESULT`, as is `result` in the postcondition of a function that returns no value; a body local named `result` is not visible to the clause and stays legal.
- [CONTRACT-OLD-1] `old(expr)` inside `@ensures` is evaluated once at entry, after the preconditions, in the parameter scope, and the postcondition reads the captured value. It is `E_CONTRACT_OLD` anywhere else — in `@requires`, in `@invariant`, in a body — nested inside another `old`, or with other than one argument. Under default value semantics, a captured collection value is an independent snapshot and `old(xs)` is accepted. Under `--legacy-sharing`, a captured value whose type is or holds a shared type (`list`, `dict`, `set`, `class`, `byte_buffer`, `Cell`) could observe the body's writes, so it is `E_CONTRACT_OLD_SHARED`, suggesting `old(len(xs))` or `old(xs.copy())`; a fresh copy — a literal, a comprehension, `.copy()`, a slice, `keys()`/`values()`/`items()` — is accepted when its elements share nothing.
- [CONTRACT-PURE-1] A contract condition — every `@requires`, `@ensures` and `@invariant` clause and each `old(...)` capture — may allocate but has no other effect. The effect pass judges it with the same inference it runs for every function: an effect of its own node or of any function it calls (`output`, `filesystem`, `network`, `clock`, `random`, `environment`, `host`, `mutation`, a callback's declared ceiling) is `E_CONTRACT_EFFECT` naming the effect and the operation it comes from. The checker also rejects, as `E_CONTRACT_EFFECT`, a `mut` call-site marker, an assignment, a collection method that changes its receiver, `?`, and a condition that would take ownership of a move-only or result-bearing value, since a condition only observes. `debug(...)` contributes no effect, and a list a literal or comprehension builds is the condition's own.
- [CONTRACT-INVARIANT-1] An `@invariant` condition, which sees only `self`, is checked wherever a value of its type is built or changed: every record construction (positional, named, with defaults), every `replace(...)`, after every field write to a value of that type (`r.f = v`, `r.f += v`, a destructuring target, and a nested `outer.inner.f = v`, which checks `inner`'s type), and every `wrap[D](v)` of a domain; a domain invariant may `unwrap(self)`. A plain copy is not re-checked. A false one traps `R_INVARIANT` at the construction or write that produced the value, naming the type, the clause text and its message.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-1] A violated contract ends the program like any other trap, with exit status 1, in every build mode and backend — `run`, `mylang test`, a Debug or ReleaseFast build, the fast frame-map protocol and `--release` (LLVM); no mode removes a contract. The runtime reports the contract's own code (`R_REQUIRES`, `R_ENSURES`, `R_INVARIANT`) where a plain `assert` reports `R_ASSERT`, the structured trap record carries the same text as its `operation`, a `--diagnostics=json` record adds a `contract` object with `kind`, `subject`, `clause`, the optional `message` and, for a precondition, the `caller` location, and `mylang test` prints `CODE: text` beside the frames.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-2] Lowering preserves assertion kind separately from message text and calls a dedicated native entry for each contract kind. An ordinary `assert` always traps `R_ASSERT`, even when its literal message starts with `R_REQUIRES: `, `R_ENSURES: ` or `R_INVARIANT: `; its full message is preserved in accounted and fast runtime protocols.
- [CONTRACT-INTERFACE-1] Contracts are part of the published interface. `mylang list-interfaces` prints each exported declaration's clauses after its signature, the `--contracts` manifest carries them as `contracts`, `mylang context` shows them above the signature, a declaration's `interface_sha256` covers their normalized text, so `api-diff` classifies a contract edit as a breaking interface change, and a `mylang test` receipt's implementation hash folds in the text of every contract clause its body checks, including an invariant written on another declaration.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-1] Every cache sees an edit to a contract clause: a function's semantic-cache region and dependency context start at its first attribute line and collect the names its clauses reference, a record's or domain's declaration fingerprint frames its `@invariant` lines, and the native object and metadata caches key on the emitted code and messages. Editing only one `@requires` makes the next `run` and `mylang test` over the same caches trap under the new clause, with no stale body, object or receipt reused.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-2] Invariant checks synthesized inside a function retain only spans inside that function's declaration region, including operator positions and generated locals. Unchanged bodies constructing invariant-carrying values can be reused after source relocation; edits to a referenced invariant still invalidate them.

## Value semantics (default, D9)

- [COW-FIELD-RETURN-1] A record field extracted to a mutable local and unconditionally stored back to the same field can transfer its payload without marking it shared when the original field cannot be observed between those operations and the temporary does not survive the store-back. Closed branches, loops and match arms, and reads through distinct sibling fields, including signed-index normalization, may occur before the store-back. A source observation, control-flow exit, external branch into the mutation or store-back region, different destination field or surviving temporary retains ordinary copy-on-write; pre-existing independent values remain unchanged.


- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-1] Value semantics is the default. For one migration release, `--legacy-sharing` or explicit `"value_semantics": false` in the entry's `mylang.json` selects legacy sharing; `--value-semantics` remains a compatibility spelling. Explicit flags before guest `--` win over inherited `MYLANG_VALUE_SEMANTICS=0|1`, which wins over project configuration. Flags apply to `check`, `check-module`, `check-project`, `build`, `run`, `run-project`, `test`, `emit-qbe`, `emit-project-qbe`, `dump-bytecode`, `fix` and `invoke`; unsupported commands or multiple mode flags exit 2. The resolved mode is carried into every child compiler by replacing the command with its flag removed and the environment variable set. Configuration values must be boolean and unique. Frontend, semantic, native fragment, manifest, replay and test-result cache identities keep the two modes separate; explicit legacy mode retains its original emitted IL.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-2] Under default value semantics a `list`, `dict`, `set` or `byte_buffer`, and every record, tuple, union payload, `Option`/`Result` or fixed array holding one, is an independent value. Assignment to a named local (a `for` variable included), a store into a record field, tuple, payload, fixed-array element, list element (`append`, `insert`, `xs[i] = v`), dictionary value (`d[k] = v`, `setdefault`), the elements an `extend`, `update` or keyed `sorted` takes from another collection, a named `match` payload binding of a value another name holds, the default a `get` or `pop` may hand back, and a `return` each give the destination its own value: lowering places one `value_copy` after every such transfer that is not fresh (a literal, constructor, call result, `pop`, `escape`), and the ownership analysis (`OWNERSHIP-ANALYSIS-1`) drops it where the transfer is a move (the source is never read again) or a borrow (neither side is written while both are read). A local that may hold a shared value — a parameter, a compiler temporary filled from a place, a local some store filled with a borrow — never hands its value over. A shallow copy, slice or concatenation of a collection whose elements hold collections copies those elements too. A read-only argument or other operand is lent, not copied: the callee cannot write it, and where a later operand of the same expression may write it, it is copied when it is evaluated instead (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-14`). Every real copy shallow-copies its outer value into private scratch and marks each directly reachable mutable collection payload shared: fixed flat values mark up to eight leaves inline, while dynamic and recursive shapes use rooted `myl_qbe_cow_transfer`. A later write clones shared storage in its payload's owning region; a pinned payload is cloned at transfer. When an inner arena is open around a write to an outer value, using the owner's region prevents the replacement from dangling at arena exit. Immutable `str` and `bytes` stay shared; `class` objects and Cells exist only under `--legacy-sharing`. A copy is not a `managed_allocation` effect in this stage, and `.api` interface files do not change.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-13] A compiler temporary filled by the last read of an owned local (`load_local s; store_local tmp` with `s` never read again, as lowering stages the right-hand side of a field store) takes that local's value over instead of naming a place, so its own last read may move it: `record.field = local` at the local's last use moves the value into the field instead of copying it and marking it shared, and a later write through the field needs no clone. A temporary filled from a place, a parameter, a local that is read again later or a local that itself only names a place or holds a borrow (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-2`) still never hands its value over.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-14] Under default value semantics the operands of one expression evaluate left to right, and each operand's value is fixed when it is evaluated. A lent read-only operand (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-2`) whose root local a later operand of the same expression may write in place — a nested `mut` argument, a mutating collection method such as `pop`, `append` or `clear`, or a store in a lowered block — is copied right after it is evaluated: `lst(xs, xs.pop())` passes the three-element list, `lst(ys, push(mut ys))` the list before the push, and `out.print(zs, push(mut zs))` prints `zs` as it was. This covers the arguments of direct, module-qualified and function-value calls (positional, or keyword arguments staged in source order), `print` operands, both operands of a comparison or other binary operator, the base of `replace(...)` and of a fixed-array read (pending writes whose receiver overlaps an evaluated `mut` argument are rejected by MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1), the arguments of a collection method, and a run of source-order temporaries such as `zip`'s two sources or membership's needle. A read-only collection method with arguments whose receiver is a named local (`d.get(k, f(mut d))`, `xs.index(g(mut xs))`) reads a copy of the receiver taken before its arguments evaluate, as `COW-READ-RECEIVER-1` already captures a receiver a compiler temporary holds. The copy only marks the payload shared under the collector or takes an owner under reference counting; the later write clones it. The check is static and per expression, following compiler temporaries to the local they read: a root no later operand can write, a fresh operand or a value without a mutable collection needs nothing, and nothing is emitted for it.
- [VALUE-BUFFER-FRESH-1] Under default value semantics `byte_buffer(values)` classifies as a fresh value, not a read of existing storage: the conversion widens the `list[int]` into octet storage of its own (`myl_qbe_bytes_from_list` allocates a new block and copies every element, trapping `R_BYTE_RANGE` on an out-of-range one), so the buffer shares nothing with `values` and storing it into a local places no `value_copy`. Soundness: the checker builds this cast only for a `list[int]` argument (`E_BYTES_CONVERSION` otherwise), so it is never an identity conversion of a `byte_buffer` and no read of the result reaches storage the list keeps; every other conversion keeps reading through to its source, in particular `bytes(buffer)`, which publishes a snapshot over the buffer's own block ([PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1]). Assigning the buffer to another name, or storing it in a record, list or dictionary, is an ordinary transfer of a place and is unchanged.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-3] Under default value semantics a `mut` parameter of a plain `record` or tuple is `inout`: the callee receives the caller's variable, so a field assignment, a nested field or collection write and a rebinding of the whole parameter all reach the caller. The argument must be a mutable local variable: `mut h.c` or `mut rows[i]` is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` ("`mut` on a record passes a variable itself; copy the field or element into a local, pass `mut` that local, and store it back") and a `for` or `match` binding is `E_IMMUTABLE`. A function with a `mut` record parameter cannot be a function value and a callback with one cannot be a lambda, as for `mut` fixed arrays; a host or C-ABI function cannot take one. `mut` on a number, bool, `str` or `bytes` is still `E_MUTATION_MARKER`, saying to return the new value instead.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-11] In both legacy and value-semantics modes an explicit `mut` parameter of a plain record or tuple passes the caller's mutable local variable by reference. This includes scalar-only records: a field write or whole-record rebinding in the callee changes the caller's value, and a call-site `mut` marker is required. A field or element cannot stand in for that local; copy it to a mutable local and store it back after the call. Legacy programs that used a `mut` marker on a record field argument must use this explicit storeback, while unmarked record calls retain their previous copy behavior.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-10] Under default value semantics, a `class` declaration is `E_VALUE_CLASS` even if unused: shared mutable identity must be expressed as a pool handle or other explicit id, while finite value trees use records or recursive unions. The builtin `Cell[T]` is `E_VALUE_CELL` both as a type in a signature or field and as a constructor, because it is a shared mutable slot; use a value record with `mut` and store-back, or a pool handle for identity. A user-declared type named `Cell` is ordinary and remains legal. Under `--legacy-sharing`, class and builtin Cell keep their previous behavior during the migration window. Arenas and `escape` continue to work for value trees.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-4] Under default value semantics a local that received a value is the function's own: writing a copy of a parameter (`ys = xs; ys.append(1)`) needs no `mut` and is not a write to the parameter, and a returned value never aliases a parameter (its return origin is fresh). A non-`mut` parameter itself stays read-only, and writing it is `E_MUTATION_MARKER` worded "read-only here; declare `xs: mut T` so the change reaches the caller, or assign it to a local (`copy = xs`) and change the copy". A write through a `for` variable (with `enumerate`, `zip`, `reversed`, `d.values()`, `d.items()`) or a named `match` payload binding of a value another name holds — `row.append(0)`, `p.x = 1`, `row[0] = 5`, passing `mut row` — is `E_VALUE_BINDING_WRITE`, naming the binding and saying to change the collection through its place or to assign the binding to a local first; under `--legacy-sharing`, a binding of a `class` or Cell stays writable, and a match on a call result binds the result itself. A write into a function's returned value (`f(x).items.append(1)`) is `E_VALUE_TEMPORARY_WRITE`. No diagnostic under default value semantics says "reachable from the caller", "use `class`" or "pass a fresh local snapshot"; a dead field write to a record copy says to declare the parameter `mut` or return the updated record.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-5] Under default value semantics `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` is never reported between a read-only and a `mut` argument: a read-only argument is a value of its own. Where one could share storage with a `mut` argument of the same call — the same root local or an untraceable temporary — lowering passes a real copy taken before the call, so `touch(mut a, a)` gives the callee a snapshot of `a`. A read-only argument evaluated after a `mut` operand may mark its payload shared; the call makes that `mut` operand unique again before pinning it, and writes the replacement back to its owning place. Two `mut` arguments of one call may not name one value or overlapping places: that is `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP` under MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1, with or without the switch.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-6] Under default value semantics, when a `mut` parameter is never written because the only change went to a named local holding a copy of it or of a path into it (`row = rows[i]; smooth(mut row)`), `E_MUTATION_MARKER` says so and says how the change reaches the caller — "store the copy back after changing it (`rows[i] = row`)", or for a copy of a whole collection parameter "change `rows` in place" — instead of suggesting to remove `mut`.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-7] Under default value semantics `old(expr)` of a value whose writable storage is all collections (a `list`, `dict`, `set`, `byte_buffer` or a record of them) is accepted: the capture is a value of its own. Under `--legacy-sharing`, a value that reaches a `class` or Cell remains `E_CONTRACT_OLD_SHARED`.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-8] Documentation examples use the default value model. `# doc: value-semantics` explicitly selects that model and `# doc: legacy-sharing` selects the migration release's legacy model, for both checking and execution, including grouped projects. The doctest runner passes the corresponding CLI mode flag to child commands; contradictory directives fail through the CLI's multiple-mode rejection.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-9] The QBE build keeps frame pointers in the value-copy runtime unit, which can report an allocation failure through `myl_qbe_fail_allocation`, so a trap from a value copy can unwind across that unit to the guest call site.

- [RC-RUNTIME-CLI-1] `--rc-runtime`, `--rc-runtime=stats`, `--rc-runtime=sites` and `--gc-runtime` choose one native ownership contract before any cache lookup. Explicit flags override the versioned `MYLANG_RUNTIME` carrier; malformed/duplicate flags and unknown inherited versions are usage errors. Project semantic-mode discovery ignores these flags, explicit RC rejects legacy sharing, inherited RC normalizes to GC for legacy projects, and child compilers inherit the resolved contract. Arguments after `--` belong to the guest.

- [RC-OWNERSHIP-PLAN-1] Experimental RC planning distinguishes physical owning edges from logical copy-on-write needs and unique resource drops. Immutable bytes and pure recursive boxes still carry lifetime obligations. Raw external resources have no RC header: their shapes contain no count edge, and their existing unique-drop and consumed-parameter obligations remain separate. Owned results are the canonical call convention, and only an explicit every-return physical certificate authorizes a borrowed result. Value versions, conditional ownership, caller storage and non-sharing scope holds are separate compiler facts; failure cleanup names precise lowering checkpoints. Canonical physical slots receive one initialization guard, and guarded local releases follow the real CFG, final reference liveness and pending operands. Selected RC metadata compiles against its versioned C ABI; RC is the default runtime (`RC-DEFAULT-1`).

- [RC-RUNTIME-CONTRACT-1] Native memory selection uses explicit versioned GC, RC and counted-RC ownership contracts; current RC uses ABI5, retired ABI1/ABI2/ABI3/ABI4 and unknown versions are rejected, inherited RC cannot change legacy-sharing behavior, and an explicit RC request with legacy sharing is incompatible. Historical native manifests without an ownership tag always mean GC, independently of the process default. Lowering reads one normalized process selection rather than independently parsing environment state. The process default is RC (`RC-DEFAULT-1`).
- [RC-DEFAULT-1] Reference counting (`rc-v3`) is the default native runtime: a command that names no runtime flag, no `--gc-stress` and no `MYLANG_RUNTIME` builds and runs with RC, and an empty carrier decodes to RC in the compiler, the native build driver, host applications and in-process test sessions alike. `--gc-runtime` or `MYLANG_RUNTIME=gc-v1` opts out to the tracing collector, and legacy sharing still normalizes a default or inherited RC to GC. `--gc-stress` among a command's own options (before `--`) selects GC over the default and the carrier, and naming it with an explicit RC flag is a usage error (exit 2); a program built for RC refuses `--gc-stress` in `MYLANG_OPTIONS` with exit 2 and a message naming `--gc-runtime`, and a stressed in-process test run builds under GC. Every cache is keyed by the resolved contract, so the default change never reuses a GC artifact for an RC build or the reverse, and historical untagged native manifests still mean GC.
- [RC-RESOURCE-COMPLETE-1] Under reference counting, `complete_resource` takes its handle over as a callee takes a consumed argument: the operand's cleanup guard is disarmed before the provider runs, so a completed handle is closed only by the provider and a refused one is owned only by the returned failure record, and no operand cleanup drops it a second time. Results, traps and cleanup order match the collector.
- [RC-UNIQUE-FIELD-TAKE-1] Under reference counting, reading a field that holds a unique drop (a resource handle, or a value holding one) out of a record operand the read consumes moves the field: it is taken into the result and cleared in the record before the record's cleanup runs, so the handle keeps exactly one owner (for example `complete_resource[Finish](failure.resource)` after a refusal). Every other field read is unchanged.

- [RC-UNIQUE-LOWER-1] Experimental RC mutations check physical owner counts through typed guarded slots. Failed detachment leaves the original owner intact and preserves its runtime status; borrowed inputs adopt only a fresh clone. Semantic COW loop hoisting remains disabled until physical temporary ownership is proved compatible.

- [RC-RECEIVER-LOWER-1] Experimental RC call and pending receiver scopes use non-sharing lifetime holds and typed pin journals. Projections borrow prepared candidates until the mutator publishes its result; normal completion restores pins before dropping holds, a return closes the frame's still-open scopes (RC-EXIT-SCOPES-1), and a trap abandons them (RC-TRAP-TEARDOWN-1).
- [RC-NARROWED-MUTATION-1] Experimental RC treats the compiler's defensive Option projection used as a mutation receiver as a borrow of its source owning edge. Detachment updates that source before the receiver is captured; ordinary named match payloads still publish independent snapshots.

- [RC-BORROW-BOUND-1] Optional borrowed-entry certification declines recursive call chains and analyses exceeding 16 active functions or 65,536 abstract state cells across one root query. Resource-bound refusal retains canonical owned entry behavior and never prevents a legal program from compiling; genuine allocator failures still propagate.
- [RC-BORROW-COMPOSE-1] A complete direct-callee physical proof maps its returned parameter anchor through the exact actual argument, preserving one caller anchor or immortals across every converged return. Pure integer, boolean and unit helper results use the same closed grammar and keep their canonical ABI. Completed immutable proofs are memoized per root query for at most 16 distinct callee bodies without eviction, sharing all depth/state/work limits; in-progress results and recursive dependencies are never reused. Full transitive proofs and resolved mappings participate in versioned caller cache identities. Canonical and indirect managed results remain owned.
- [RC-BORROW-LOOP-1] Borrowed-entry loop certification reaches a bounded monotone CFG fixed point before inspecting every reachable physical return. A backedge that introduces another parameter anchor declines certification. Unavailable locals remain distinct from initialized immortal values and cannot regain availability at a join. State work is capped at 524,288 abstract cells across transfer, join and final ordered hashing; exhausted proofs use canonical owned entries. Dependency identities are computed in source order after convergence.
- [RC-BORROW-ENTRY-1] A separate internal borrowed-result entry requires an every-return physical certificate anchored in one read-only parameter. The certificate admits field and list or fixed-array projections, bounded control flow whose converged returns share the same parameter anchor, and inline fallback values proved to contain only immortal edges. Calls in certified bodies must transitively prove mapped parameter-anchored or immortal managed results, or a closed scalar result, without mutation or fresh heap allocation. Fresh heap wrappers, box materialization, different parameter anchors and unknown control flow decline certification. Alternate entries have separate symbols and source frame maps. Its identity includes the ownership ABI, physical result shape and accepted projection body; a callee edit that invalidates the certificate invalidates borrowed callers. Canonical, indirect and exported entries retain owned-result conventions. A direct borrowed caller establishes any independent result ownership while its argument anchors are still live.
- [COW-RECEIVER-ARGUMENT-1] A selected nested mutation receiver stays pinned through evaluation of later arguments and through its consuming operation. A callback that snapshots any owning ancestor receives an independent finite pending path; normal completion and early propagation restore its pins before leaving the frame.
- [COW-MUT-ARGUMENT-1] A nested `mut` collection argument retains its selected writing place while later arguments are evaluated. Its copy-on-write replacement is published into that place, read-only same-call arguments remain independent snapshots, and field/key/index evaluation is not repeated. Normal and failed calls release all temporary pins and holds.

- [RC-BORROW-CACHE-1] RC caller fragments include complete physical borrowed-entry certificates, including transitive immortal helper bodies and the ownership ABI. A missing certificate selects the owned ABI. Until a complete certificate can be restored from a stand-in record, RC lowers all physical bodies before certification while retaining unit-text and native-object reuse; GC stand-in behavior is unchanged. Warm emission after certificate changes is identical to fresh emission, and alternate entries retain separate source metadata.

- [RC-ADMISSION-1] Unique arguments consumed on entry are closed and their counted children released even when frame admission or heap-frame allocation refuses before the callee journal exists. Refusal cleanup uses existing incoming ABI storage and bounded scratch, including packed signatures. Indirect calls transfer these arguments exactly once. A heap-frame try allocation leaves trapping until after cold argument cleanup; entry accounting checks run with initialized RC guards.

- [RC-TEMPORARY-EDGE-1] On finalized empty-stack fallthrough edges after a local store, RC may release a dead compiler temporary that owns a managed collection pointer. The release is guarded and excludes parameters, address aliases, recursive boxes, inline aggregates, unique resources and certified borrowed entries. Pending receiver paths retain their own lifetime holds and their implicit parent/key uses remain in final liveness. A live borrowed receiver keeps its physical owner anchor even after its mutation scope has ended. Named snapshot owners and live derived values retain their independent ownership.

- [RC-READ-LOAN-1] A single-definition compiler receiver used only for scalar collection reads may borrow a stable named source while its full live range contains only scalar operations and those reads. The physical ownership plan retains the source anchor through the loan. Calls, mutations, arena transitions, managed results and dictionary hold roles retain ordinary snapshot publication. Bounded analysis refusal preserves that fallback.


- [RC-READONLY-BODY-1] A closed physical proof may borrow all managed values throughout a canonical function activation when its result has no ownership edges, its parameters are read-only, and every instruction excludes allocation, mutation, unique resources, arenas, escaping values and unknown calls. Self-recursion preserves the same caller anchors. Such a body needs no ownership journal or reference-count traffic, while native frame limits, bounds checks and failure traces remain active. Any instruction outside the proved grammar retains ordinary RC lowering.
- [RC-READONLY-CALLS-1] Canonical read-only activations may compose direct guest helpers only when every transitive helper satisfies the same closed grammar and returns no ownership edges. Managed-result, host, indirect, mutating, allocating and cyclic helper dependencies are refused; existing self-recursion remains supported. A bounded completed proof hashes the root and transitive helper bodies, native layouts, grammar and runtime identity into the RC caller fragment key. A changed or refused helper proof invalidates caller reuse; GC keys and the canonical call ABI remain unchanged.

- [COW-READ-RECEIVER-1] A collection read captures its receiver value before evaluating its index or other arguments. A later callback that mutates the source cannot change that captured value or the length used to normalize a negative index. Capturing a pinned receiver creates a private copy; writing receivers obey MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1. GC and RC preserve the same evaluation order and results.
- [COW-ITERATION-IDENTITY-1] A managed iteration source retains its lifetime without adding a value snapshot unless the loop body rebinds its root local. A live place, including a fixed array, is re-read each step regardless of payload sharing; a named copy taken before the loop stays independent. Fresh sources remain owned by the loop, and callback-index reads still snapshot their receiver.
- [D1-LIVE-PLACE] Iteration over a place rooted at a local or parameter sees element writes and mut-callee writes through that place, including record-field write-back, fixed-array places, comprehensions and generators. Produced expressions, filters and nested clauses retain the same live-source rule. Indices are evaluated once at entry; every indexed managed ancestor's length is checked exactly each step, including ancestors of immutable str/bytes. A live list or byte buffer sees growth and traps a decrease from its last observed length, including after earlier growth; dictionaries, sets and immutable str/bytes terminals trap any observed length change. Same-length str/bytes field replacement is visible; text length and indexing count Unicode scalars. Fixed-array terminal lengths are static. Direct rebinding of the root in the body selects an independent entry snapshot; non-place sources keep their entry behavior. Guards run before each element and after each continued or final iteration, while break exits without another guard.

- [RC-INERT-RECEIVER-1] A compiler-only list receiver can omit its temporary hold and pin scope when a bounded closed forward region proves that one stable ordinary owner remains live through its sole consuming scalar mutation. The region permits scalar computation and descendant receiver reads but no callback, owner replacement, managed publication, arena boundary, backward edge or alternate entry. Uniqueness checks and all trap paths remain unchanged; declined regions retain full receiver scopes.

- [RC-NESTED-READ-LOAN-1] A managed collection read may lend its result directly into a single-definition compiler receiver when the complete projected chain is anchored by one stable named root and every receiver interval contains only scalar operations and further certified reads. Named rows, published results, callbacks, mutation, arena changes and dictionary hold roles retain owned publication. The physical plan keeps the root until the final scalar read; bounded or cyclic proof refusal retains ordinary ownership.
- [RC-PLAIN-READ-LOAN-1] An inert collection receiver may remain physically borrowed through a terminal read of an inline value with no counted or unique edges. Result bytes are copied before the receiver anchor ends. Callbacks, mutation, arena transitions, uncertified managed or unique results and unknown effects retain the existing guarded capture; intervening operations remain scalar-only.
- [RC-LOAN-RELOCATE-1] Storing an operand that holds a live private loan into a compiler temporary, or moving such a temporary onto the operand stack, relocates the loan: its bytes and loan role move to the destination and the source guard and bytes are cleared, so the hold count never changes. An owner is still taken, and a source with no token or another role still acquires a fresh loan before its old token is dropped; an operand a plain `load_local` pushed just before the store carries no token, so it skips the loan test.
- [RC-SELF-STORE-1] When the ownership analysis elides a match binding's payload copy (`load_local x; value_copy payload; store_local x`), the store puts the same bits back into `x`. While `x` holds an ordinary owner the RC lowering skips the store, which retained the value again and released the owner it replaced; a binding holding a loan, a hold or no token still takes the ordinary store.
- [RC-RETURN-MOVE-1] `return x` of a named local of the return type (not a parameter, not by reference or caller storage, with its own journal entry) moves `x`'s owner into the result when `x` holds one, instead of retaining a copy and releasing `x` at exit. A local holding a loan, a hold or no token is still copied from its anchor, and other returns keep the ordinary operand transfer.
- [RC-TRANSIENT-FIELD-1] A field read of a value record (not a class body, collection or box) that was pushed by a plain local read, or by another such field read, publishes no token when the very next instruction consumes it and is not a branch target: another field read projects out of it at once, and a real value copy of the same type acquires its own owner from its bits at once. Nothing between the two instructions can write the local or release what it holds, so the loan (one hold per edge of the read value) is gone; every other consumer, a live view result and any read of an owned or loaned operand keep their loan.
- [RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1] A read-only parameter (the `read` call contract) that the function never stores, moves, references or rebinds is lent for the whole activation, so every value read out of it stays valid without a token: a field read, a list element read of a list local anchored this way, and an elided value copy of such a value publish no loan, and a compiler temporary or named local whose every definition stores such a value (a store, which copies a value wider than one word in by its bits, or a match arm binding whose scrutinee is such a value) is bound as borrowed from the parameter's storage. Its arms bind the payload in place without a retain, and the elided self store after an arm binding is skipped. A copy the copy-on-write planner records as an alias of such a parameter is still only read and may be bound the same way. A local that is moved, referenced, mutated, iterated, used as a receiver or place, aliased by another local, a live view, or defined by any other value keeps its ordinary ownership, and every consumer needing an owner copies from the borrowed bits.
- [RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-1] A compiler list temporary filled straight from a field chain of a `mut` record parameter (`load_local r; get_field...; store_local t`) and otherwise used only for its length and element reads (a counted element takes its own loan as it is read) is borrowed from the parameter's storage, and its field chain publishes no loan, when no instruction in the temporary's live range stores, moves, references (passes `mut`), binds or place-writes the parameter or crosses an arena boundary. The caller owns the parameter, so calls that do not receive it cannot replace or release the list. Any other use of the temporary, a unique-resource element, or a write of the root keeps the loan.
- [RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1] A field read out of a `mut` record parameter (`load_local r; get_field...`) publishes no loan when straight-line code consumes the read value before any instruction stores, moves, references (passes `mut`), binds or place-writes the parameter, crosses an arena boundary or branches. A consumer that needs an owner copies from the bits, and a call that receives the value as a read-only argument borrows it while the caller keeps the parameter unchanged; a later `mut` use of the parameter, a branch or an arena boundary before the consumer keeps the loan, except that a read a later operand's `mut` use may write is copied when it is read (`VALUE-SEMANTICS-14`) and so publishes an owner instead.
- [RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-2] The straight-line consumer of an `RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1` field read may be a length read (`len(record.items)`, `len(record.buffer)`), which replaces the value on top of the stack with its scalar length at the same height; the read then publishes no loan either. Every other condition of `RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1` is unchanged.
- [RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-2] A list the frame can write — a `mut` list parameter, or a named list local that is no place, alias or iteration source — anchors the values read out of it while nothing writes it. A local whose every store takes a value read out of such a root (an element read of the root or of a local bound to it, carried by plain field reads, elided copies and domain wraps over a forward region no branch enters) is bound as borrowed from the root's storage when no instruction that can write the root runs while the local is live and, for a named local root, the root is live wherever the local is (a dead root's lowering may hand its token to a capture). The root is written by a store, move, drop or reference of it, a last read of a named local root (which may hand the list to a new owner), a mutating operation, copy-on-write check or loop-entry check on a local rooted in it, a call handed a `mut` reference into it, an arena boundary and, for a named local, a return. A root is refused outright when an instruction that writes it can run with a value read out of it, out of a bound local, or of unknown origin on the operand stack; otherwise every read out of it and out of its bound locals publishes no token, a bound local's store copies bits without a token, and a consumer that needs an owner copies from the bits. Pattern bindings are never bound.
- [RC-BORROW-CALL-ANCHOR-1] A local every store of which takes the result of a direct call whose callee has a borrowed-entry certificate anchored in an argument that is itself anchored in a never-written read-only parameter is bound as borrowed from that parameter (`RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1`), exactly as the call's own result already publishes no owner (`RC-BORROW-MATCH-1`): its store copies the result's bits without a retain, and a consumer that needs an owner copies from the bits. Binding computes each callee's certificate once; a body proved raw and readonly is unaffected.
- [RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-2] A store into a local whose operand was pushed earlier than the instruction just before it — the first argument of an inlined call, stored after the arguments pushed above it — counts as an `RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1` definition when that operand is a read of an anchored local (its whole value, an element or a field chain) carried to the store only by plain field reads, elided copies and domain wraps over a forward region no branch enters from outside. The local is then bound as borrowed like any anchored local; every other store keeps the immediate-predecessor rule.
- [RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-3] Under value semantics, MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 guarantees disjoint accepted mutable arguments, so another mutable record parameter of the same type alone does not prevent RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-1 or RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1. Writes to the root and the remaining lifetime refusals retain their loans. Legacy sharing always lowers for the collector and does not use RC anchors.
- [RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-4] Under value semantics, MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 guarantees disjoint accepted mutable arguments even when their types equal, hold or are held by one another, so that type relation alone does not prevent RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-1, RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1 or RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-2. Root writes and other lifetime refusals retain their tokens. Legacy sharing always lowers for the collector and does not use RC anchors.
- [RC-ANCHORED-SHARE-1] A copy transfer's RC share (the owner a semantic copy publishes so a later write clones) is skipped when the instruction it precedes stores the value into a local the RC plan binds as borrowed from a physical anchor (RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1, RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-1, the read-loan chains). Each anchor proof shows nothing writes the anchor's value while the local is live and the local itself is never written, so the copy is unobservable; the anchored store keeps only the bits and would otherwise leave the published owner in the operand journal, where a later typed drop of that journal slot released the wrong value (`frame.items[touch(mut frame, 7) % len(frame.items)]` crashed once OWNERSHIP-MUT-RECORD-WRITE-1 made the receiver a copy).
- [RC-MUT-PAYLOAD-SNAPSHOT-1] Under RC a `value_copy` of a whole `mut` list, dictionary, set or byte buffer parameter (`snapshot = table`) is always a real copy, even where the ownership analysis proves it a borrow. An elided copy still stored an owner into the named local, so a later write through the parameter whose receiver is captured before its key reads the snapshot (`table["k" + str(len(snapshot))] = 7`) found two owners and cloned the payload into the callee's own parameter slot, detaching it from the caller's value: the caller never saw the write. The real copy publishes the snapshot the way any copy does and the parameter stays the caller's payload. A store the RC plan binds as borrowed from a physical anchor takes no owner, so the copy stays elided there (RC-ANCHORED-SHARE-1); publishing one would strand its owner in the operand journal. The collector's lowering is unchanged.
- [RC-BORROW-MATCH-1] The borrowed-entry certificate admits `match`: each arm binds its payload with the scrutinee's parameter anchor (or none for a payload without counted edges) and no arm falls through, so an accessor such as `match rt.objects[i]: case Obj.string(s): return s` returns the payload borrowed from its read-only parameter. A direct call through such a certificate whose anchored argument is itself anchored (RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1) yields an anchored value that takes no loan; consumers needing an owner copy from its bits. Arms binding unique resources, slots that are parameters or of another type, and calls whose anchored argument cannot be traced keep the owned convention.
- [RC-PLAIN-UPDATE-CALL-1] Inside the interval of a `mut`-record-rooted borrow (RC-INTERVAL-ANCHOR-1, RC-OPERAND-ANCHOR-1), storing the root back from an update of only count-free fields of its own value (`r.ip = r.ip + 1`), or passing the root `mut` straight to a direct call whose body (and every call it passes the parameter on to, six deep) writes that parameter only this way and never mutates, moves or passes `mut` a field read out of it, does not end the borrow: no counted field of the root changes identity. Any other store, move, binding or `mut` use of the root, an unrecognized argument or update shape, recursion beyond the bound or a body over 1024 instructions keeps the loan.
- [RC-INERT-COUNTED-RECEIVER-1] A list receiver filled from a field chain (or alias) of a stable owner, as `RC-INERT-FIELD-RECEIVER-1` and `RC-INERT-RECEIVER-1` admit it, also needs no receiver scope, pin or backing hold when its list's elements own counted edges, provided the interval ends by storing (`xs[i] = v`) or appending one element that a plain local read loads, and copies, right before the write, every earlier interval instruction is one the plain proof admits, and the element type is safe (no resource, callable, capability, live view, class, Cell or unique drop) and cannot reach the receiver's own list type through its edges. Publishing the new element and releasing the one it replaces then cannot reach, share or release the receiver, whose root keeps its owner throughout. The capture's uniqueness check stays; element steps, a value computed inside the interval, calls and every other shape keep the scoped protocol. GC lowering is unchanged.
- [RC-PLAIN-UPDATE-CALL-2] The RC-PLAIN-UPDATE-CALL-1 summary treats every local projected out of the `mut` record parameter (a field chain, an element or entry read, an array element, a copy of the parameter, or any value reaching the store along a branch), transitively, as the parameter's own storage: mutating one in place, moving it, passing it by reference, or loading a collection-typed one in a body that calls a function or callback with a writable parameter writes through the root, so the call keeps the caller's loan. A nested write (`frame.rows[0].append(x)`) or a `mut` field or element argument (`push(mut frame.rows)`) can clone a pinned receiver and release the list the caller's borrow names.
- [RC-INERT-PLAIN-UPDATE-1] A bounded inert list receiver interval may contain an inline record update when its result and every consumed operand have no counted or unique edges and its record is not managed. The update writes private result scratch without changing receiver or index storage. Existing stable-root, closed-control-flow, callback, arena and publication checks remain required; managed fields and unique finalizers retain the scoped protocol.

- [RC-INLINE-TAKE-1] RC token transfers for bounded fixed layouts with ordinary pointer leaves may relocate directly after all headers and disjoint source/destination ranges are checked. Pinned, arena, dying, held, unique-resource, and unsupported inline layouts retain the generic transactional path. Header masks are compared as full-width values before branching. Direct moves change no counts, perform no fallible work or callback, and preserve public-boundary drain ordering and source guards on fallback refusal.
- [RC-OPERAND-STAGE-1] RC lowering never exposes an operand stack word's address. An owned one-word operand that an ownership helper or relocation check reads or clears through an address is first copied into its own frame cell (`%rcstageN`), and a one-word scalar element a runtime append reads through an address is copied into a private argument cell (`%rcarg`); the helper operates on that cell. Operand words are then only loaded and stored, so QBE and LLVM keep them in registers exactly as they do for the collector. Ownership, failure and cleanup behaviour are unchanged; the operand word is dead after its value is consumed.
- [RC-PLAIN-CONSTRUCT-1] An RC inline record constructor whose record type owns nothing (no counted field and no unique drop) writes its fields straight into the aggregate, with no zero fill and no cleanup-journal entry for the partial value: no step of it can fail, and a plain value has no cleanup. Records that own something keep both.
- [RC-PLAIN-READ-IN-PLACE-1] A borrowed plain (non-owning, non-scalar) result is copied into private aggregate storage before its inputs are dropped only when one of the consumed operands can own storage whose cleanup could clear those bytes. An element read from a list held in a local consumes only its index, so the result keeps pointing at the element, as in the collector; the following store or projection copies or reads it.
- [RC-ELEMENT-BORROW-1] Under RC a local filled only by list element reads may hold the element's address instead of a copy (`QBE-ELEMREF-2`) when the element owns nothing and the list is read through a local no release can reach while the frame runs: a hidden iteration source (its hold spans the loop), a list parameter the function never rebinds (the caller owns it), or a temporary every store of which is an adjacent capture of one of those. The borrow's live range must also be RC-inert: no return, and no store, pop, projection input, constructor, update, element read or call result that owns anything. Element reads of any other list, and every call-result or payload borrow, keep their copies. The empty-frame certificate treats such a local as the plain local it is.
- [RC-ELEMENT-BORROW-2] Every RC lowering of a store into an element-borrowed slot keeps the element's address, including a raw readonly body's own local stores; no lowering copies the element into the one-word slot.
- [RC-ELEMENT-BORROW-3] Under RC a local filled only by list element reads may hold the element's address even when the element owns references, when the list is a parameter the function never writes or mutates in place (or a temporary every store of which is an adjacent capture of one) and the RC plan binds the local as borrowed from that parameter's storage (`RC-READONLY-ANCHOR-1`). The caller keeps such a parameter and everything it reaches alive and unchanged for the whole activation, so the borrow needs no inert live range: returns, calls, allocation and releases may run while it is live. The local is never an owner: its stores keep the address, its reads push it, and no loan, drop or copy touches it. A `mut` list parameter, a hidden iteration source, a list local, and any local the anchor analysis does not bind keep their copies.
- [RC-GLUE-1] RC ownership operations on counted inline types (loan, unloan, physical retain, take and drop) run through compiler-generated per-type glue instead of runtime metadata lookup and shape interpretation: records are flattened, fixed arrays loop, variants switch on their tag, and each pointer edge is a null test, a mark test and one count update. A one-word value runs its fast path inline and calls its glue only for an arena edge or a last token. Loans and physical retains complete whole-value count-capacity admission before acquiring any token; near-limit acquisitions retain the exact transactional runtime fallback. Releases drain only after queueing a death; arena, pinned and overlapping cases retain the runtime entry required by each operation. Owner and hold counts use the ABI5 unsigned64 limit while every token remains a stored pointer-sized word. Counting builds route every operation through the glue, which records the runtime's events and checks its invariants. Static frame drops (RC-STATIC-DROPS-1) release an owner through the type's drop glue and a loan or one-word held pointer through its unloan glue. List element writes of a collection's element type (set and room append, including appends of plain inline aggregates), view hold acquisition and uniqueness checks of an already-unique payload also run without a runtime lookup.
